A project whose `.woodcore` states its `layout.pdf` keeps it, and a folder converted from the old layout states the place its old build wrote. ⚠️ **Accepted price**: the deliverable is no longer where the workbooks are; the archive keeps the PDF out of its zip, its flat copy standing beside it; and the Musterprojekt project still carries the PDF its last export wrote at its top, left where it is rather than deleted.
- *[in force]* **A word math does not know is quoted by asking math, settled (v0.87).** The engineer: «pourquoi dois-je écrire `G_(1,\"Ed\")` et pourquoi `G_(1,Ed)` ne fonctionne pas ?». `quoted-words` quotes every run of letters **`dictionary(math)` does not carry**, rather than a list written by hand of the words a structural engineer types, which would have been a second list to keep and would have missed the next word (§10quinquies). ⚠️ **What it gives up, stated**: a word math *does* know keeps its meaning, so `c_(pi)` prints c_π and not c with «pi», and a letter followed by digits is split, `gamma_(M1)` into M and 1. Either is written with its quotes as before where the other reading is meant. The rule is idempotent, so no symbol already right — none of the export's — moved.
- *[in force]* **`*` is read as a multiplication in the formula string, not by a show rule, settled (v0.87).** The engineer had put `show math.star: $dot$` in his `config.typ` and it did nothing to `#eq`: a show rule rewrites content, and the formula of `eq` and `verify` is a **string** until the engine evaluates it, which is also where its units are computed. So `star-as-dot` reads `*` as `dot` in the string itself, where both the evaluation and the printing see it, and leaves a quoted `*`, an escaped one and an attachment (`F^*`) alone. Accepted price: an asterisk meant as one between two operands is written by its name, `ast`. A static model's or a schema's label typed with `*` is not a formula, and since the second half of v0.87 its figure draws the `*` as `·` where the text is drawn (§3.3octies); a `*` in math written by hand between dollars stays Typst's asterisk, a show rule turning it into a dot reaching the engine's own `F^*` too (§10quinquies).
- *[in force]* **`#distance` reads the norm's own files from Typst, settled (v0.87).** The engineer asked for the tables of minimum distances of SIA 265 printed with the diameter given, «Tableau 24, 27, 28, 32, 33, 36, 37, 38, 39, 40, 41». Chosen over two alternatives: exporting the tables into `data/` (a table printed for a diameter the report states is not an export of the project) and restating the multiples in Typst (a second copy of norm values, which §2 rule 1 and v0.77's rule forbid). `lib/distance.typ` reads `calc/norms/*.json` by the relative path `../calc/norms/`, the files `calc/norms/sia265.py` checks — **one source for Python and Typst** — so the engine copy in each project carries those fourteen files as well as `lib/`, and `examples/` freezes them (§12, §10sexies). ⚠️ `connector` takes **English keys and the German names** of `connector.TYPES` (the engineer's choice), so a call can name the kind Woodcore's connector carries; `staple` and `threaded-rod` are refused with the reason. **`alpha:` was added** to his signature, Tabelle 41's spacing along the grain depending on it; **ρ_k was not**, since it decides which table applies and changes no value, and is said in the norm's own footnote. ⚠️ **Accepted price**: the arithmetic that turns a multiple into millimetres lives twice, in `lib/distance.typ` and in `sia265.distance`, and `tests/test_distance.py` holds the two equal cell by cell; and the list of files is named in four places, held equal by the same test.
- *[in force]* **A negative number is printed with a minus sign, set where a number becomes text, settled (v0.87).** «Un signe moins doit être affiché.» Changed in `signed-text`, `prose-minus` and the figures' `figure_number`, and **not in `fmt-decimals`**, whose output is also math source, a registry value and SVG coordinates, each read back by something that does not read U+2212 (§4bis). Accepted price: `prose-minus` also turns a hyphen before a digit in the engineer's own Excel text into a minus, where it stands as a sign would.
- *[in force]* **A load's colour leaves its label black, settled (v0.87).** Five colours, red the default and saved by leaving `color` out (§3.3octies). Colouring the label too was the obvious reading and was set aside: the label is text on a white box, read the same whatever the arrows' colour, and colouring it would have changed every figure already drawn. Accepted price: which label belongs to which load is read off its place, not its colour (§14).
- *[in force]* **A highlighted point is ringed over the pieces, settled (v0.87).** «Un point doit être affiché au-dessus de tout.» A second marker, `<!-- highlight-points -->`, after the pieces and before the markers, where the disc at the first marker lay under pieces filled at three quarters opacity (§3.3decies). A **ring** and not a disc, since a disc over a piece hides the piece round the point, and sized from the drawing rather than fixed. Accepted price: a piece's, a line's and a dimension's halos are still fixed sizes (§14), and a figure exported before v0.87 still takes every halo at the old marker.
- *[in force]* **The program's version is bumped with every release, settled (v0.87).** `project.VERSION` had stayed 0.83.0 through v0.84, v0.85 and v0.86, so `engine_state` took the installed v0.83's engine copy for one of the sources' own edited between builds (*stale*) rather than for an *older* one — and the engineer, writing an exercise with the installed program, reported as missing `#eq(label:, descr:)`, which the sources had had since v0.85.2. It is **0.87.0**, both real projects had their engine replaced, and `engine_state` checks only the files a copy's `VERSION` lists, so a copy older than `lib/distance.typ` reads as older and not as edited (§12). The comparison between copies is exactly as good as that number.
- *[in force]* **Level 3 says everything a value carries, and the four arguments are on all three commands, settled (v0.85.2).** The engineer, reading v0.85.1 back: «le level 3 doit donc afficher le label, la description, la norm et la norm-ref — il faut rajouter la description pour être complet», and «les fonctions var, val et eq doivent avoir comme arguments label, descr, norm et norm-ref afin de pouvoir donner ces arguments ou les écrasés s'ils existent déjà». Two halves, and the second is the one with a decision in it. ⚠️ **Where each part goes was settled already and is not interchangeable**, so completing level 3 meant putting the description in the one place it belongs rather than after the label on the line: the label is what the variable *is* and is read with it, after an em dash (v0.61.1); the description is why *this* value was chosen and is §9's footnote; the citation is the right-hand column's, which is what keeps it level with the line it cites (§10). Inline there is neither an em dash nor a column, so the three fold into one footnote, which `val` has done since v0.80. ⚠️ **The description is level 3's and not level 2's**: a label names a number and is wanted as soon as the number is shown, an explanation of a choice is what a checker reads. That moved `val`'s choices from 2 to 3 with the description they explain, which is the one thing v0.80's row had to be rewritten for. ⚠️ **`descr:` is an argument again**, and this is v0.80's rename corrected rather than reversed: that version renamed `#var`'s `descr:` to `label:` on the ground of «one word for one notion» (§4bis) — right about the word, wrong about there being one notion, which is what v0.85.1 found out from the other end. ⚠️ **Overriding is the point of the four, not declaring them**: a var of the export arrives with a label, a description and a citation of its own, and a report quoting it for something narrower has to be able to say so at the call. What is not overridden is kept, field by field — `#var("ce", var-of(…), norm: "SIA 265")` keeps «Expositionsbeiwert» and takes the new citation.
- *[in force]* **`label` and `description` are two fields, and one level scheme for `var`, `val` and `eq`, settled (v0.85.1).** The engineer read the Bezeichnung column of `#find(object: "variable")` and asked one question — where is that value kept in the data? — with one opinion after it: it should be the label. He was right, and the question was the only way the defect could have surfaced. In the data `label` and `description` have always been two fields (§3.1, §9); in the **registry** they were one, because v0.80 renamed `#var`'s `descr:` to `label:` and left the field called `description`. So a var object read out of the export had its label dropped and printed its choices sentence in its place, and `find`'s column had to look in both fields to show anything at all — a fallback whose own comment claimed the two were the same thing, which a compile disproves. ⚠️ **It was invisible for five versions because nothing printed the two side by side.** `find` is the first thing to put a var of the export and a var of the report in one column, and that is the argument for the command existing at all, more than the listing it was written for. ⚠️ The second half is the engineer's scale for the three commands that print a single value: **1 the value, 2 the label, 3 the citation.** `val` already read that way since v0.80, so only `var` and `eq` moved — and the move exposed a second thing nobody had looked at: **`#var` printed its citation in the right-hand column at *every* level**, so a client report carried «SIA 265 §6.2.1.1» beside a bare number and nothing saying what the number was. Chosen over leaving `eq` out of the scale, which was the other reading of «pour #var, #val et #eq»: `eq`'s two formula lines are its *working* and stay at 2 and 3, and a label and a citation are extras that sit on the same scale as everybody else's. ⚠️ **Accepted price**: a level-1 or level-2 page that carried a citation loses it — the engineer's own `Sections/005` prints `#var("R_d", domain: ("connector", "VU-SD10"))` with `SIA 265 §6.2.1.1` beside it today — and `test_report`'s annex-citation probe had to move to level 3, its subject being the wording of a reference and not the level it appears at. ⚠️ And a **publisher** of registry records states a `label`: `show-polar-moment`'s seven published vars said `description`, which under the split would have blanked `find`'s column for exactly the command the engineer was reading.
- *[in force]* **Level 0 is the one level that prints nothing, and every other level prints something, settled (v0.85).** The engineer's rule, in two sentences of his own: «au level 0, il ne doit pas y avoir d'affichage», and «lorsqu'un level:1 fait en sorte que rien ne s'affiche, alors il faut que ce soit plutôt level:0 — tout autre chose que level:0 doit toujours afficher quelque chose» (§3.7, §4, §10quinquies). Until v0.85 a declaration was hidden with `level: 1`, which is the *client* report, so one scale carried two notions and they were confused in exactly one direction: `#var("k_dens", 0.806, level: 1)` said «hide this» and «this is what a client sees» with the same argument, and a hand-written variable was therefore missing from the one report where the bare answer above it is most nearly the point. Chosen over the two alternatives. A **flag** (`print: false`) says the same thing in a second vocabulary, and `var-line` would then carry both a flag and a level to say which of its items are printed. And **shifting the scale down** — 0 the client, 1 the engineer, 2 the checker — renumbers §3.7's three reports, every `--input level=`, every frozen example page and Woodcore's own 1 · 2 · 3 selector, for a word. ⚠️ **Accepted price, and it is the one thing that changed on a page nobody asked to change**: `#var` and `#var-unit` now print at level 1 what they print at level 2, so 12 of the frozen `-L1` example pages gained the declarations that used to vanish at that level (§10sexies). That is the rule working rather than a side effect — a declaration that belongs on no page says `level: 0` — and the engineer's own `Sections/005` was migrated with it, its five `#var(…, level:1)` becoming `level:0`, which the *old* engine reads the same way and so could be written before the engine moved. ⚠️ **A level outside auto, 0, 1, 2 and 3 is now refused by all six commands**, where five of them checked nothing at all: `level: 4` was read as 3, and `level: 0` — the very argument this version gives the engineer — had three different meanings depending on which command was given it.
- *[in force]* **A frozen set carries every input the test restates, settled (v0.85.1).** `tests/fixtures/verbindung.json` froze the cells of the engineer's `Verbindung.xlsm` the dowel and norm tests compare with (v0.81, §11ter), and it froze the results and *some* of the inputs — the ones somebody judged to matter. The day he entered a force-grain angle, C19/D19 being outside the set, the test whose whole job is to say «the workbook moved» passed, and a different test went red on a number: the suite reported the wrong thing about the right event. The rule now is mechanical and needs no judgement — **every input the Python case restates is frozen**, 25 of them — and the case is written to restate as little as it can, reading the rest from the cells. ⚠️ The two are one decision rather than two: freezing an input and reading it are what let the *engine* be exonerated in minutes when the numbers move. With the angles put in, k_β, R_d and R_d,Verb reproduced the sheet to the digit, so the question became which of the test's assumptions was stale rather than whether SIA 265 was being applied wrongly. ⚠️ Accepted price: a wider set is a wider surface for the engineer's own edits to trip, and `test_the_workbook_still_holds_the_frozen_cells` will now fire for a species or a predrilling he changes and not only for a result. That is the point — it fires **where the change is**, and the docstring says re-freezing is deliberate, like `build_examples.py --freeze`.
- *[in force]* **A command for writing a report rather than for the finished page, settled (v0.85).** `#find` prints what there is to name in a call, and its output is not meant to be delivered — one calls it, reads it, deletes the call (§10sexies). It is the only command here of which that is true, so it was worth asking whether it belonged in the engine at all rather than in Woodcore. It does, on the engineer's own ground: the loads tab reads `data/calculations.json` and nothing else, so a connector's three dozen vars, a polar moment's geometry and — above all — the forces computed in Typst were listed in no window and no file. What the window cannot show, the report must be able to ask for. ⚠️ Accepted price: a command whose two compiling examples and category are carried for output nobody ships, and one more name in every section's scope. ⚠️ Its `object: "domain"` lists `connector` and `connection` beside the four calculation domains, where `domain-ids()` lists only what `calculations.json` holds and says so in its own header — a command written to answer «what is there?» has to be complete, and a new one can be without widening a promise made elsewhere.
- *[in force]* **The name one types and the symbol the page prints are two columns, settled (v0.85).** The engineer, on reading `find`'s first table: «le nom_variable et le nom_affiché (pas toujours le même, non ?)» — and he is right, `q_k11` is set q_(k,11) and `f_u_k` is set f_(u,k). A table read in order to write a call has to carry both. `type: "expression"` drops the symbol column, its value cell already printing it.
- *[in force]* **`#var` reads a `domain:`, and a pair falls back to its instance's var-domain, settled (v0.85).** Asked twice, a month apart in feel: first «est-il possible de remplacer var-of par la commande #val ?» — no, `val` returns content and `var` needs the object — then «est-ce que #var(… var-of(…)) peut retourner la même chose que #var(… domain(…)) ? Comme ça, il y aurait un fonctionnement similaire en les différentes commandes», which is the right question and is what was built. ⚠️ The two shapes are read in **different places**, and that is the whole of the design: a pair is a plain json read, so it is made where `var` builds its record; a string is a `var-domain`, which lives in a state, so its record is built inside the update, which is handed the dictionary itself. The refusal is raised in the **context** and not in the update, because the function of an update runs only where something reads that state — a panic written there is silent in a document that never looks the registry up again. ⚠️ The fallback exists because `find` and `var` disagreed without it: the table listed F_max under `("connection", "VU-MP-1")` and `#var` refused it under the same pair. The export wins a name both carry, being the traced one.
- *[in force]* **A double rule is a scope function, not a style name, settled (v0.85).** `#eq(style: "double")` is handed to the eval through its `scope:` rather than spliced into the math source like `"bold"` and `"underline"`. Two nested boxes drawing a bottom rule each, and not two nested `underline`s: an underline decorates the text runs it finds, so inside an equation it comes out under the units and not under the numbers between them — the finding `verify` had already recorded, met a second time. Keeping it inside the one eval is what leaves the spacing around every `=` math's own, where a box concatenated after the equation would have carried text spacing into it.
- *[in force]* **A polar moment publishes its forces, and the update is emitted before the context, settled (v0.85).** The engineer's own instruction — «enregistre beta_1, beta_2, Fmax, F1, F2, F3, F4 dans des variables afin qu'ils soient affichés par #find» — which is Option A of the three put to him earlier answered his own way: the display command publishes what it computed, rather than a data-access function beside it. One arithmetic, two doors, and `polar-distribution` is where it lives (§3.3quinquies). ⚠️ The version shipped it emitting the update from *inside* the context and he found the defect within the day; what it cost is recorded in §3.3quinquies rather than here, because the rule it breaks — read a state and write it from one context — was already written down in §10quinquies and had simply not been applied to a `show-*`. ⚠️ A published var carries no citation: the method is cited where the method is shown, the value is untraced.
- *[in force]* **A run of calculations is announced by a line of its own, settled (v0.84).** The engineer read the level 3 v0.84 had just written out and said «le level 3 est trop brouillon», naming the page to copy: Bemessungsbeispiele zur SIA 265, p. 291. What makes that page readable is not its formulas — they are the same ones — but that each run of them is named before it starts («Charakteristische Lochleibungsfestigkeiten für C24:», «Holzdicken:»), so the eye finds the block it wants without reading the symbols. That is a **`heading` on a var**, printed by `show-calc` as a row of its own (§3.3, §3.3nonies). Chosen over the two things that already existed and nearly fit: a `group_label`, which is set *inline* before the first value of a row and cannot head a run of formulas; and a `tables` entry used for its title, which is the wrong shape and, since v0.84's figure plates, already means something else. It is the same family as `bold` and `inline` — a property of the calculation, because which runs a reader groups is something the calculation knows and the display cannot — and it generalises: the snow and wind reports would take it unchanged. ⚠️ **Accepted price, and it is a trap rather than a cost**: a heading is printed where its var's own first row is printed, so one hung on a var the level hides, or on one absorbed into an intermediate formula, is dropped in silence and its block goes unnamed. It caught three of them while this was written, which is why `tests/test_connectors.py` now walks every heading of every assembly and refuses a dropped one. ⚠️ A second price, smaller: a grid row cannot be kept with the next, so a heading can be left at the foot of a page with its block overleaf.
- *[in force]* **The book's layout, and Anhang A's formulas, settled (v0.84).** The engineer's «présente les t_i,erf et k_β comme dans le livre» is read as the *layout*, not the formulas. p. 291 is the «vereinfachte Berechnung nach Ziffer 6.2.1» — Tabelle 23's simplified k_β — and this module has computed the more accurate Anhang A form for every configuration since v0.68, at his own choice (§13, v0.68). Printing Tabelle 23's formulas beside Anhang A's numbers is a page whose formula does not produce the value next to it, which is the one failure this display layer exists to prevent (§10bis, v0.20). So the blocks, their names and the pieces' own words are the book's, and the formulas under them stay Tabellen 45 and 46's. ⚠️ If the *numbers* of the book are ever wanted, that is a change to `analyse()` and a decision to take out loud — not a change to the printout.
- *[in force]* **The norm's formulas are written out where a table held their results, settled (v0.84).** Tabelle 22's embedment strengths, Tabellen 45/46's thicknesses and k_beta corners and Tabelle 25's stiffnesses are chains of steps now, and the three tables are gone (§3.3nonies). The engineer asked for it in those words, three times over. Chosen over keeping both — a formula above and a table of the same numbers below is one place too many for the same fact (§3.3ter's own argument for dropping the two Sigma formulas) — and over a table carrying a `formula` line, which `render-table` already supports and which says the formula **once** for a table of many rows, where these are one formula per member evaluated with different numbers. ⚠️ **Accepted price: level 3 gains lines.** Nine formulas where there were three tables. It is bounded by two things the engineer asked for in the same message: every one of them is `inline`, so each is one row, and the runs of single values around them are now one row each — the project's own connector went from 34 rows to 28 while gaining the nine.
- *[in force]* **Tabellen 45 and 46, not Tabelle 23, settled (v0.84).** The engineer wrote «reprends les formules du tableau 23 de la sia265» for the thicknesses and the k_beta factors. Tabelle 23 is the **simplified** k_beta that 6.2.1.1 offers as an alternative to Anhang A, and this module has computed the Anhang A form for every configuration since v0.68 — at the engineer's own choice, recorded above. Printing Tabelle 23's formulas beside Anhang A's numbers would be a page whose formula does not produce the value next to it, which is the single failure this whole display layer exists to prevent (§10bis, v0.20). So Tabellen 45/46 are what is written out, and it is flagged here and in `dowel.record`'s own docstring rather than done silently: if the engineer wants the *simplified* k_beta he wants a different calculation, not a different printout.
- *[in force]* **`inline` is a property of the step, not a setting of the report, settled (v0.84).** «Rend les calculs moins étalés verticalement», with a list of which lines to join. Chosen over a document-wide switch and over a `level`-dependent rule: whether a formula is short enough to be read across one line is something the calculation knows and the display cannot, and equation (88) itself — the long one — stays on two rows in the very same record. It is the `bold` mechanism's own shape (§3.3), for the same reason. ⚠️ A **bounded** formula is never inline, having no numbers line to join; and where the substitution would say nothing new — `k_(beta,1,2) = k_(beta,max)`, whose numbers *are* the answer — it is left out rather than printed twice, which is `substituted-line`'s own rule applied to the joined form.
- *[in force]* **A var may keep its row although a formula absorbs it (`keep`), settled (v0.84).** `show-calc` has left an intermediate step's operands off rows of their own since v0.20, because the substituted formula already shows them. Writing SIA 265's tables out as formulas turned the diameter, each member's density and each member's thickness into operands, and every one of them would have vanished from the page — which is the opposite of what the engineer asked for, his own example line being «Bauteil 1: ρ_(k,1) = 350 kg/m³; t_1 = 50 mm». Chosen over dropping the absorption rule (it is right, and the snow and wind reports rest on it) and over marking those steps `bold` (which would make them answers and set them bold). ⚠️ α is deliberately **not** kept: it shows where it acts, inside f_h,α,k and inside k_red, which is the engineer's own line.
- *[in force]* **R_d is computed first and R_d,Verb from it, settled (v0.84).** «Calcule d'abord R_d … puis calcule R_d,Verb = n_tot · R_d. Affiche d'abord le R_d, puis le R_d,Verb.» Equation (88) states R_d,Verb with n_tot inside it; the product without n_tot *is* R_d, so nothing is approximated either way and the two numbers are unchanged to the last digit (`tests/test_dowel.py` holds both against the workbook). What changes is which of them carries the working, and the engineer reads the resistance of one fastener first. A **truss node reduces both** for the same reason: the report gives both answers, so both have to be reduced, and neither is folded into the other (5.3.6.2.2 being narrower than the word «truss», v0.68).
- *[in force]* **eta_w is applied to each member's K_ser, settled (v0.84).** «Il faut toujours multiplier le résultat avec eta_w, comme tu le sais.» It was on the connection's K_ser,Verb (v0.79, AUD-2-3) and the product is identical; what the move buys is that the formula Tabelle 25 states is printed **with eta_w in it**, and K_ser,Verb is then a count times a stiffness, `n_tot · p · K_ser`. The per-member values are unchanged in Feuchteklasse 1, which is the class both workbook cases carry, so `test_dowel`'s cell-by-cell checks needed no edit.
- *[in force]* **K_ser,Verb is printed at levels 1 and 2, settled (v0.84).** Under R_d,Verb, and **not** by adding it to `results`: `results` are the resistances a verification reads and what `show-calc` sets bold, and a stiffness is neither. `show-connector-resistance` pushes it after the results instead, guarded, so an export written before v0.84 and a frozen documentation page that never carried it do not panic.
- *[in force]* **A `tables` entry may be a plate of figures with no grid, settled (v0.84).** What was left of the k_beta table once its numbers became formulas is SIA 265's Figur 45, one per thickness (v0.77, the engineer's own request). Chosen over a mechanism of its own — `render-table` is where a table's `level` and `place` are honoured, and a second path would have to repeat both — and over keeping the table beside the formulas, which is the duplication this version removed. `columns` and `rows` are empty lists, `connector.figures` still finds its `curves` there, and `validation._check_tables` needed no change.
- *[in force]* **A one-column `group` names that column, settled (v0.84).** «Dans la cellule tout en haut à gauche (cellule vide) indique le diamètre «d = 10mm»»: every distance of Tabelle 24 is a multiple of d and the table said nowhere which d. Chosen over a new field on the table (a `corner`, which only this one table would ever use) and over typing it into the first column's own header, where it would sit under the group line rather than on it. A group over one column is therefore set at **that column's** alignment rather than centred, goes through `render-prose` so its label may carry math, and takes **no rule** under it — there being nothing for a rule to say about how far a name over a single column reaches.
- *[in force]* **The dowel layout box comes before the dowels box, settled (v0.84).** «L'encadré Disposition des broches doit se trouver avant l'encadré Broche (renommer en Broches)», and «l'encadré Broches doit reprendre le nombre de points sélectionné». The two are one decision: the pattern is drawn first and n_tot is read off it, so the box that settles the count stands before the box that spends it. ⚠️ The field is **read-only while it follows the drawing**: `draft()` has taken n_tot off the ticked markers since v0.74 while the spin box showed whatever was loaded, so the form said one thing and the calculation used another — and a field one may type into that is then ignored is worse than a field one may not. *Écraser le compte du dessin* frees it, which is the one way the two ever differ.
- *[in force]* **A lit result states its own ink, settled (v0.84).** «Lorsque des résultats sont mis en évidence dans le volet de droite, ils ne sont plus lisibles.» The ground was set and the text left to the palette, which is near-white in Windows' dark theme; lighting a row was making it harder to read. Both are set now, and held by the test that already watched the highlight. The same rule the window applies to every other colour it writes (§10quater's swatches take the palette's ink for the opposite reason).
- *[in force]* **Where a member's strength class came from is the description of its rho_k, settled (v0.84).** It was the note under the Lochleibungsfestigkeit table, a **list of sentences** since v0.79 (AUD-3-7: one string with every piece's provenance in a hole came back half German in a French report). With the table gone it had to move, and the var it is about is the density that strength class gives — one sentence about one var, which is what AUD-3-7's rule asks for and a stricter form of it than a list on a table.
- *[in force]* **A step's name carries no glossary entry, settled (v0.84).** No surface prints one: `show-calc` renders a step's *formula* (§10bis), Woodcore names its results from its own `RESULT_NAMES`, and `docs/typst-reference.xlsx` is not translated. The per-member names the glossary already holds from v0.68 are harmless and are left alone; the ~50 that v0.84's new steps would have needed were not added, and `tests/test_i18n.py` says so where it checks them — for the doubled «Scherfuge» of AUD-3-4 and for nothing else.

- *[in force]* **The positions table opens level 3, and a formula shares its row with its own numbers, settled (v0.84).** Both halves of one request of the engineer's, about `show-polar-moment` (§3.3quinquies). x_c and z_c are the means of the x and z columns of *Lage der Verbindungsmittel*, so they are printed under that table rather than above it — which costs one thing, stated here rather than left to be discovered: with `title: none` the citation now stands beside the rotation centre, under the table, a table read from the data carrying no norm of its own and so having nowhere to put one. And a declaration and the working behind it are one statement, so they are set on one line, joined by the semicolon `show-calc` already joins a `group` with (§3.3). Chosen over the two other ways of gaining the same height: setting these lines smaller or tightening their gutter, which would make a calculation read differently from every other line of the report, and dropping the letters line, which is the one that makes the numbers line checkable (§10bis, §2 rule 1). ⚠️ Accepted price: a joined line is about twice as long, so on a connection carrying five-digit forces it reaches the citation column and, past it, wraps — ragged, `show-polar-moment` setting `justify: false` for exactly that reason.
- *[in force]* **A table is three rules and nothing else, settled (v0.84).** Asked for by the engineer in the shape of the Typst he wanted to read — `stroke: none`, an `align` per column, a 1 pt rule above the headings and under the last row, a 0.5 pt rule under the headings — which is the booktabs arrangement, the way a printed norm and a paper set a table (§3.3). It is **one edit**: `render-table` is the only `table()` in the whole project, every other tabular thing here being a `grid`, so the layer build-ups, SIA 261's Tabelle 8, the wind's six forces, a connector's four tables and a polar moment's three all changed together and no section file was touched. Two things came with it and are what make it more than a change of stroke. **A column is aligned by what it holds**, which is what a table without vertical rules needs: with the grid gone, the space between two columns is all that parts them, and a number read from the right lines its digits up down the column, where left-aligned numbers of unequal length read as a ragged block. And **a group's name carries a rule spanning exactly its own columns**, since a vertical rule is no longer there to say how far the name reaches. ⚠️ Accepted price: a `math` column is centred whether it holds a symbol or a number, and SIA 261's Tabelle 8 holds numbers in one — right-aligning them would align their **footnote markers** rather than their digits, which is why the one rule serves both rather than the kind being split in two. ⚠️ The 187 frozen example pages were regenerated (13 moved, §10sexies); `data/` did not move, so the export is unchanged to the byte and every number in the report is the number it was.
- *[in force]* **Woodcore's own copy of a table follows the report's, settled (v0.84).** `connectors_tab.record_table`'s docstring has promised since v0.77 that its two tables are «laid out as the report lays them out», so the alignment and the rules were carried over rather than left to make that sentence false. Doing it turned up a rule that **had never shown**: the totals' rule was set on `<tr style="border-top:1px solid">`, and Qt's rich text honours a border on a **cell** and ignores one on a row — measured here by rendering both forms through a `QTextDocument` into a `QImage` rather than by reading the docs. It is set on the cells now, and «Minimale Abstände» and «Verschiebungsmodul» have the rule the docstring always claimed for them. ⚠️ The window keeps no outer rule: a panel is already a box, and the two thick rules would draw a second one inside it.
- *[in force]* **The start page, and a project opened in a process of its own, settled (v0.83).** The plan's design, carried out: the page opens a project in its own process, which has none yet, and every other opening starts a new process, the window closing itself first through `closeEvent`. Chosen over switching project in place, which would have needed every module and every tab to forget one project and read another — the price v0.81 declined; the start of a new process, 7 to 8 s from the sources, is what it costs. The engineer's daily start changes: `Woodcore.bat` with no argument opened the last project straight away until v0.83 and opens the start page now, the last project first and selected, Enter opening it. *Nouveau projet* and *Ouvrir dans une nouvelle fenêtre* leave the window open, as Word does; *Ouvrir* and *Fermer le projet* replace it.
- *[in force]* **The lock is `<name>.woodcore.lock`, and it is advice, settled (v0.83).** Named against the plan's `~$<name>.woodcore`: NextCloud's default exclusion list carries `~$*` (checked in this machine's `sync-exclude.lst`), and a lock the other machine never receives protects nothing. It reaches the other machine when NextCloud has synced it, and a window that crashed leaves it behind: a stale lock of this machine is taken over by the process check, another machine's is shown with its time and left to the engineer's judgement — *Lecture seule*, *Ouvrir quand même*, *Annuler*. Accepted price: two machines opening one project within the same few seconds both get it, and the command line does not look at it.
- *[in force]* **Read-only is enforced once, on the classes, settled (v0.83).** Every way an editor tab writes a source — `save`, `delete`, `on_moved`, `create_plans`, and `source_archive.Actions`' `archive`, `restore` and `purge` — is wrapped at import by `refused_when_read_only`, before any window is built, so the buttons and shortcuts connect to the guarded methods; a guarded method is called with no more arguments than it takes, a Qt signal handing a slot what it carries. Chosen over a check in each of the five tabs' twenty-odd write paths, which a sixth tab would forget, and over disabling the tabs, which would have made a project opened read-only impossible to look through.
- *[in force]* **A project's empty folders live in its `.woodcore`, settled (v0.83), replacing v0.75's `settings.json`.** A folder is a property of the sources it files, and a folder kept in the user's settings showed in every project's list. `gui.empty_folders` (`katalog.empty_folders` until v0.96, still read), written through `project.update`, which rewrites the file whole beside itself and moves it into place and keeps every key it is not given. The folders `settings.json` held were given to the first project opened by v0.83 and taken out of the settings.
- *[in force]* **The Typst-Katalog's settings and caches are Woodcore's, taken over once, settled (v0.83).** `%APPDATA%\Woodcore\settings.json` and `%LOCALAPPDATA%\Woodcore\`, copied and moved from `typst-katalog` the first time Woodcore starts without its own, the old folders left where they were; never while `WOODCORE_SETTINGS` points elsewhere, which is a test run. The window's words, its title, its message boxes and its icon say Woodcore; the domain modules' English messages and this document called the window «Woodcore», the engineer's word, until v0.96, when he asked for a name common to French, German and English and everything followed (below).
- *[in force]* **«Importer Excel» goes through `woodcore-cli`, settled (v0.83).** `export --data-only`, the command line's own route, where the window ran `export_all.py` itself: installed, there is no interpreter to run a script with, and one route from the sources and from the installed program is one route to keep right.
- *[in force]* **A new project carries no load workbook, settled (v0.83).** The office's `Belastungsannahmen` is a template with macros and ActiveX controls openpyxl cannot write, and a workbook written by it would be one the engineer's Excel no longer fills as expected; §3.3's rule that a project without one is a blocking error stood until v0.89.1, when a project that has never had one became a warning (below). `Positionen.xlsx` is made, its headers the export's own.
- *[in force]* **No load workbook is a warning while no export has read one, settled (v0.89.1).** The engineer asked for a warning rather than a stop; of three rules put to him — always a warning, a setting per project, or a warning only where no export has read one — he chose the last. The reason §3.3 made it a stop is kept where it applies: a workbook renamed out of the pattern, moved into a subfolder or not yet synced by NextCloud on the other machine looks exactly like a project without loads, and a warning there would let the export write the report's loads away, a new project's sections then printing their headings over nothing. `data/project.json` already names every workbook an export read, so the rule costs no new field: a load workbook named there and gone stops the export, naming it. Chosen over always a warning, which is one line and loses that protection, and over a setting in the `.woodcore`, which a new project would still stop on until it was ticked. Accepted price: the rule rests on the last export's record, so a project whose `data/project.json` was deleted is warned about and not stopped — which is also the way out the stop names for a project that deliberately gave up its loads. The warning is the export's, in English, and Woodcore shows it in its «Export terminé avec des avertissements» box. ⚠️ **No longer a warning since v0.117** (the engineer, 07.10.2026: «cette erreur ne doit plus être affichée. Dans le futur, les actions seront directement calculées par woodcore»): a project that never had a workbook is exported with one line of information on stdout, `export_all: no load workbook in … -- loads: none`, beside the line naming the workbooks read, and nothing in the window's box of warnings; `registry.no_workbook_note` is gone. Accepted price: the rule «a missing input warns» (CLAUDE.md) is held by the report alone here, whose load domains print empty; the stop for a workbook the last export read and that is gone is unchanged.
- *[in force]* **An old folder is converted by naming it, not by moving it, settled (v0.83).** *Convertir un ancien dossier* writes a `.woodcore` whose `layout` points at the folders where they are. Chosen over moving them into the new layout, which step 4 did by hand for Musterprojekt with a reference to compare against — a conversion that moves an engineer's files needs that, and a button cannot give it. The engine such a folder carries is `unknown` to `engine_state` — engine files with no VERSION naming them — and asked about before it is replaced, where it would have been `absent` and overwritten.
- *[in force]* **The installed program ships its sources, not a frozen archive of them, settled (v0.83).** PyInstaller freezes the modules it finds into an archive whose `__file__` is not the module's place in the checkout, and the program derives a dozen paths from its own files and has three packages without an `__init__.py`. Shipping `calc/` as it is under `_internal/`, imported from there, keeps every one of those paths and every import as the sources have them, and makes the frozen program the same code the suite runs; what `calc/` imports from outside is read off its import statements, those inside functions included, so nothing imported late is missing. Accepted price: the program's Python is readable in its install folder, which for a program whose repository is shared with developers is no price.
- *[in force]* **The build environment is isolated, settled (v0.83).** A virtual environment holding `requirements.txt` and PyInstaller and nothing else, where the first try layered it over the Python the report runs on: what is frozen is then exactly what the two files pin. The first build met an obsolete `pathlib` backport in that Python's site-packages, which PyInstaller refuses to build beside, and uninstalling a package from the engineer's interpreter was not the build's to do. Accepted price: PySide6 downloaded once more into it, some 250 MB.
- *[in force]* **One installation per version, per user, and a launcher that picks, settled (v0.83).** The plan's design. An AppId per version is what lets two stand side by side; `%LOCALAPPDATA%\Programs` and `HKCU` are what let it install without an administrator; the launcher is **one file**, so an older installer run after a newer one cannot leave half of each (Inno Setup keeps the newer by its version resource), and it is the last version uninstalled that removes it with the association. The launcher prefers the engineer's sources where told to, then a pinned version (`woodcore_version`, a prefix), then the newest — and says itself, rather than through a window that opens only to refuse, when a project was exported by a newer version than any installed.
- *[in force]* **The move, and the code's history cleaned rather than restarted, settled (v0.82).** Three folders — `Musterprojekt/`, `Woodcore/`, `ExternalFiles/` — where there was one, and `git filter-repo`, run in a clone, keeping every path of the code, renamed to the root, and dropping from every commit the reference library, the workbooks, the project's report, sources, export and archives, the architect's plans and the examples' snapshot of them. Chosen over a new repository with one commit, which would have lost the history this file cites version by version, and over the history as it was, which would have handed the norms under licence and the client's data to every developer the code is shared with. The repository as it stood is kept whole as `ancien-depot-v0.82.bundle`, verified with `git bundle verify`, and its `.git` was renamed `_ancien-git/`, not deleted: a PDF delivered before v0.82 is traced through them. Accepted price: every commit's hash changed, so a hash quoted in an audit before v0.82 names a commit of the bundle; and the examples' snapshot and pages entered the new history only at the move, their plan backgrounds blank pages.
- *[in force]* **The engine's copy is guarded, settled (v0.82).** Its states and what an export may do with each (§12) were chosen over overwriting always — a report compiled after an update may print otherwise, with no word said, and the copy is what makes a project reproducible — and over never overwriting, which would keep a project on the engine it was created with. A copy a newer program wrote is never replaced: an older program writing its engine into a project a newer one exported would downgrade it silently. The files are read-only, and a copy edited by hand is its own state, *edited*, rather than one more *stale*: an edit is a decision, and it is shown before it is lost.
- *[in force]* **The record of an export carries no date, settled (v0.82).** `data/project.json` names the program, its revision, Typst and the workbooks' hashes, and nothing that changes when nothing else does. Chosen over a timestamp, which would make every export differ from the one before it and every comparison of `data/` — the reference of each step of the move, the audit's reproducibility check — say «changed»; the date is the archive's name. `git describe --dirty` says whether the code had been modified since its commit, which a version number cannot.
- *[in force]* **One zip per archive, settled (v0.82), replacing v0.71's folder.** A folder of hundreds of files met Windows' 260 characters a few folders deep — where Explorer cannot delete a file and Excel refuses to open one — and NextCloud synced it file by file. A zip is one file, its members' names carry no path of the machine, and it is a third of the size. Accepted price: an archive is opened before it is read.
- *[in force]* **`woodcore-cli` replaces `build.ps1` and the `Makefile`, settled (v0.82).** Both spelled the paths of the one project they sat in; a program that serves several projects names the project on its command line. One module, `calc/cli.py`, and the window's menu builds its flags through its `input_args`, so the two routes cannot spell the flags two ways — the accepted price of v0.62, paid off. Accepted price: the `make` targets are gone; nobody on this project had `make`.
- *[in force]* **The title page and the PDF's metadata are the project file's, settled (v0.82).** The project's name, number, client, address, company, author and checker are optional fields of its `.woodcore`, exported into `data/project.json` and read by `project-info()` and `woodcore-document`. Chosen over placeholders typed into `Statischer Rapport.typ`, one more place for a project's name to be written twice. The keys are English, as every other contract here, where the plan sketched French ones.
- *[in force]* **`.gitattributes` marks the binary files, settled (v0.82).** Git for Windows sets `core.autocrlf` for every repository, and a PDF written by pypdf holds no NUL byte, so git takes it for text and would check it out with CRLF, moving the offsets its cross-reference table points at — the examples' plan pages would no longer open. PDF, PNG, JPEG, the workbooks and zips are declared binary; a `.bat` is CRLF, which cmd.exe needs.
- *[in force]* **The program and the project come apart: Woodcore, settled (v0.81).** The engineer's request: the code and Woodcore as a program installed once, opening on a start page, a project opened from a file of its own and switched like a Word document, so a folder of structural work holds the project alone. Taken with the engineer as a set of decisions, each with its price. **An installed `.exe`** (PyInstaller in a folder, an installer per user, `typst.exe` inside), **built on demand**, while the daily mode stays the code run from its sources — the price is a build to keep working, which a check on the example project holds. **The engine is copied into the project** beside its export (Option A) rather than installed as a Typst package (Option B): a package cannot read the project's files, so the engine would need a reader passed in and, `var-of` staying context-free, a factory returning ~100 commands — every call shape changed and Tinymist's hovers probably lost — for a gain the copy already gives, the project then carrying the engine that produced its PDF. **One project per window, and another project opened in a new process**, the window closing through its own `closeEvent`: robust and already tested, at the price of a start of 7 to 8 s. **Only the code in git**, to share with developers; the project lives in NextCloud alone. **The history is cleaned** (`git filter-repo`) rather than restarted, the old repository kept apart as a bundle: the norms under licence and the client's data must not leave with the code. **The names are the engineer's**: Woodcore, `.woodcore`, and in a project `Statischer Bericht (typst)/`, `Woodcore-Datei/`, `_woodcore/`, `_Archiv/`; beside them, `Musterprojekt/`, `Woodcore/` and `ExternalFiles/` in this folder.
- *[in force]* **A project is named by its `.woodcore` file, and one process works on one, settled (v0.81).** The file carries its name and, optionally, its `layout`; a path in it is relative to the file's folder and may not leave it, and the export must lie inside the report's folder, Typst reading nothing outside it. Its keys are English like every other contract here, and `schema_version` is pinned. `project.current()` is process-wide rather than passed to every function: one window is one project, so a module asks for a path at the moment it needs it and nothing is fixed at import. Chosen over threading a project argument through some three hundred call sites, and over keeping module constants computed at import, which would have tied a process to whatever project existed when its modules loaded — the tests alone move between projects, with `project.using()`.
- *[in force]* **The example project is a real project anonymised, without its workbooks, settled (v0.81).** The engineer's choice among three: a full copy with the workbooks (every test everywhere, but workbooks derived from third-party templates and the project's figures shared), a synthetic project written by code (nothing sensitive, but some 660 lines of readers to mirror and a second workbook to maintain, which the tests deliberately avoid), or this. The same coverage as before on the engineer's machine; on a clone, the few checks that read a workbook skip, as they did. The cells `test_dowel` and `test_norms` compare with are frozen once, `tests/fixtures/verbindung.json`, so those run everywhere.
- *[in force]* **An example of the commands tab needs no project, settled (v0.81).** `examples/` is its own Typst root with a `config.typ` of its own, and a preview is handed to Typst on standard input. Until then the examples imported the report's `config.typ` and the preview wrote into the report's folder: the documentation, which is the program's, depended on the one report that happened to sit beside the code.
- *[in force]* **One command prints one value: `val` absorbed `fmt`, `inline-value` and `live-load`, settled (v0.80).** The engineer's own question — «est-il possible de n'utiliser plus que val et de supprimer fmt et live-load ? Je ne devrais rien perdre en fonctionnalité» — and the answer was yes, on one condition that turned out to be his own too: `val` takes a **`domain:`**, a string for a domain of the report and a pair for a calculation and its instance (§10quinquies). Without it the deletion would have cost brevity — `#val(var-of("live-load", "A1", "q_k"))` for `#live-load("A1", "q_k")` — and with it the shorthand is exactly as long as the command it replaces. What the four commands had between them is what `val` takes: a name in the registry, a name in a `var-domain`, a name in the export, a var object, a bare number. Accepted price, three of them: Woodcore's copy buttons and the reference workbook say `val` where they said three different things, so an older report edited by hand needs those three calls rewritten (Typst says «unknown variable» and names the call, and `tests/test_formula.py` holds that); `show-input` stays, because a *block* row whose citation stands in the right-hand column is not something inline content can be; and the `fmt-decimals` / `fmt-number` / `fmt-value` primitives stay as internals, since every table cell and every check prints through them.
- *[in force]* **`val`'s parts are `val`, `unit`, `val+unit` and `expression`, settled (v0.80).** The engineer renamed them: `"number"` became `"val"` and `"number+unit"` became `"val+unit"`, and `"expression"` is the new one, `s_k = 1.729 kN/m²`. The old names are **refused** naming the four, with no alias — which is what every rename here has done since `show-fastener-forces` (v0.32).
- *[in force]* **`val` prints its extras as one footnote, and its levels only govern them, settled (v0.80).** Level 1 the value, 2 the label and the choices, 3 the citation. The engineer's scheme, with one change made in answering it: **inline, a label cannot be set after an em dash and a citation has no column**, the citation column being a grid and therefore a block — which is the very reason `#var` breaks the line. So both go into one footnote, inside a `var-line` and in a sentence alike, which is §9's own rule for a description and its choices. Accepted price: a value quoted in a level-3 report carries a footnote marker unless the call says `level: 1` — and inside `$...$` that marker is set as math and loses its superscript, so a value quoted in an equation is written `level: 1`.
- *[in force]* **A unit written over is said on the page, in orange, settled (v0.80).** `#val("R", unit: "N")` prints 7000 N and adds an orange footnote — «Die Einheit ist für die Darstellung überschrieben: der Wert steht in N statt in kN» — whose **marker** is orange too, the engineer's own request. The orange is the one Woodcore writes an overridden value in (`override-colour` / `OVERRIDE_COLOR`, held equal by `tests/test_constants.py`), so the window and the report say "written over" in the same colour. Chosen over saying nothing (the number printed is then not the number the registry holds, and nothing on the page says so) and over refusing the conversion (it is exactly what an engineer wants when a moment is read in Nmm). ⚠️ A unit of **another dimension** is still refused: nothing is computed here, so it is a typo at the call site, not a formula disagreeing with its declared unit (v0.63's red line, which is the other case).
- *[in force]* **A row of declarations is a container, not a flag on each command, settled (v0.80).** `var-line[ … ]` sets several declarations on one line; `#var(..., inline: true)` was the alternative the engineer proposed and it was set aside for three reasons, all of them things a flag cannot do (§10quinquies): the engine writes the separators, so a value hidden by its level takes its separator with it; a `;` is never typed after a `#var(...)`, where Typst swallows it; and the row carries one label and **one citation in the right-hand column**, which is a grid and unreachable from inside a line. Accepted price: one more state (`var-line-state`) that five commands read, and a row whose items are hidden one by one can still leave an empty line — which is why `level:` on the row prints nothing at all rather than an empty grid.
- *[in force]* **`descr:` is `label:`, settled (v0.80).** The engineer's instruction, in his own words: «everywhere in the commands where I used "descr", change it to "label"». One word for one notion (§4bis's own rule, B10), and it is the word every other command already uses. `#var` is the only command that had a `descr:`; the field it writes stays `description`, which is the data's own name for it (§3.1).

The engineer's option (a) of the audit's B2, over stopping the export (the v0.33 deadlock: Woodcore could not repoint what the export stopped on) and over printing what is left (a polar moment over three fasteners of four is another calculation, printed under the same name).
- *[in force]* **A warning the window shows is a code with values, and the export's sentence stays English, settled (v0.79).** `engine.Note` is a `str`, so nothing that prints, joins or exports a warning changed; Woodcore writes its own sentence by code and never parses the English. Chosen over the report's glossary, which is keyed by German where these sentences are English, and over three languages in `calc/domains/`, which would put the window's words in the pipeline. A `ValidationError` is not converted: it names a defect in a file, for whoever repairs the file.

Accepted price: `EL+AL-Wand` is exported and not printed today — `prompt-audit.md` checks at every audit that each exported instance is named or looped over.
- *[in force]* **The η_w and γ_M/η_M written by hand in `Sections/005` stay, settled (v0.79).** The engineer's words: n_w comes from SIA 265 Tabelle 4 and SIA 265/1 for wood-based materials, γ_M/η_M from SIA 265 Tabelle 1 and SIA 265/1, «et c'est en ordre comme cela — ce calcul ne changera pas». A known exception for `prompt-audit.md`, not a defect (audit B6).
- *[in force]* **A point ticked «connecteur» is a fastener to every module, settled (v0.77), reversing the same version's «a connector takes markers only».** The engineer's words: «des points doivent toujours pouvoir être repris par d'autres modules (pas uniquement Moments polaires)». The argument for markers only was that a connector's calculation reads the fastener's kind; it reads the connector's own `type` and never a marker's, so nothing stood behind it. One function, `schema.fastener_places`, is what every module offers; a tick left off keeps a point a point to draw by.
- *[in force]* **Figur 45 is drawn by Python, word-free, and set under the table it is read with, settled (v0.77).** Chosen over drawing it in Typst, which would be a second plotter beside the SVG ones and could not be previewed; and over a figure per language, which a drawing carrying only symbols and numbers does not need (the section's own rule). The curves travel on the k_β table and leave as `figures` once drawn, so the table's rows stay the one place the numbers are. ⚠️ Accepted price: a very thin and a very thick member on one joint crowd one figure's lower corners together; the labels step into rows and a leader says which tick is whose.
- *[in force]* **Every norm value a Python calculation uses lives in `calc/norms/`, once, flat, named after its table or clause — formula coefficients included, settled (v0.77), reversing v0.68.** The engineer's rule, in those words: all JSON that restates a norm in `calc/norms/`, named `NomNorme-AnnéeNorme-TableauOuSection-designation`, no duplicated value, `calc/norms/` the only source of truth for Python (not for Excel). Three things followed. The sub-packages became flat files and one reader per norm, the name carrying the table or the clause so a value is found by the norm's own number. **v0.68's line — «a number the norm gives a symbol lives in norms/; a coefficient inside a formula stays beside it» — is reversed**: a 0,082 in `dowel.py` is a norm value written a second time, whatever shape surrounds it; the formula's *shape* stays in Python and its printed string is composed from the JSON's numbers, so the two cannot drift. And the restated lists went: `validation.MOISTURE_CLASSES` and `CONNECTOR_SPECIES` became functions reading Tabellen 3 and 22, and the `510` typed in three places became SN EN 14592's one file. ⚠️ Accepted price: a formula is read across two files, and a glossary-keyed German sentence composed from a coefficient must format it exactly as before (`%.1f` for k_red's «1.0»). ⚠️ The file for SN EN 14592 names no clause: the value is the engineer's, the edition not in the library.
- *[in force]* **Nom · Description · ID in every tab, the folder no longer a field, settled (v0.77).** The engineer's rule, in those words. The accepted price is a connector's `variant`, `length` and separate `label`, which stay in the data as the file has them (the label folded into the description on save) but are no longer edited anywhere; and a saved object whose ID is not its name's slug keeps its ID when named, which is deliberate — a rename is a decision, and a name typed is not one.

Chosen over widening the SVG's reach, which made the figure's frame, its render and its paint larger for lines a checker never reads past the frame.
- *[in force]* **A schema rename is carried into what names it, settled (v0.77).** A schema is named by its id and by nothing else, so rewriting every `schema` equal to the old id is exact. Chosen over a verbose trace, which would cost while nothing is wrong.

Chosen over keeping it as an optional annotation, which would be a field nothing computes with and a warning nobody acts on. What the two calculations of one joint share is the schema they are drawn on, which is where both have taken their geometry since v0.74. Accepted price: the report prints one line less, the connector's description under a polar moment's heading, and a reader who wants it has `show-connector` beside it. ⚠️ A 3.x file is **read as 4.0 rather than refused**: the conversion drops two fields and fills a name, and writes nothing.

Accepted price: a reference can no longer be edited in the window, only in the file.
- *[in force]* **An element's b and h follow its position and stay written over when typed, settled (v0.75).** The engineer's choice among three: read at every export off the position's `dimension` and overridable (chosen), typed by hand only, or copied once when the number is picked. It is the connectors' reference pattern (v0.68/v0.70), for the same reason — *Importer Excel* is all it takes — and it costs §7's rule that a `dimension` is never parsed, relaxed in exactly one place, `schema.parse_section`, and leniently: a text that is not b/h gives no section and a warning, never a stop. - *[in force]* **The band is the side the view shows, and `width` is gone, settled (v0.75).** One number less to keep in step: with b and h known, a separate drawn width would be a third place for one of them. Placed on the middle of the longest stretch no other piece covers, sampled along the length, the later of two equal stretches winning — the only placement that is clear of the node on the joints this project draws without the engineer dragging every label. ⚠️ Accepted price: a dimension drawn across that stretch can still meet the label (the `Beispiel` schema's does); the label is draggable, as it always was.

The section is still in the report's own text and the drawing is at its real size, so nothing is unmeasurable, but the figure alone no longer showed it -- until v0.75, whose label «T001 C24 100/200» gave it back (§13 v0.75).
- *[superseded in v0.76]* ~~**A polar moment still names its connector, settled (v0.74).**~~ **Reversed in v0.76** (above): the engineer meant the stronger reading this entry left open. The reason the plan gives is *«il ne dit pas de quel type de connecteur il s'agit»* — a statement about **fastener type**, which a polar moment has no use for, and which is satisfied by the geometry coming from a schema's markers whatever their kind. What `connector` states is what the calculation is **of**: it composes the file's own id, `<connector>-<variant>`, and `show-polar-moment` prints it under its heading at every level — and the plan's own last line keeps those signatures and those ids fixed. Dropping it would have changed the report's output to satisfy a sentence whose stated reason it does not touch. ⚠️ If the engineer meant the stronger reading, it is a small change in one direction: the id composition, `link_problem`, and the connector line of `show-polar-moment`.

The move made them two numbers where they had been one by construction — the reference member's grain *was* x — and each is what one thing the report prints is measured against: θ_(r,j) against `local`, a force-grain angle against `axis`, the force's components being in the schema's frame. Chosen over the two alternatives. **Rotating the schema into the reference member's frame** on the way in keeps one number and the old invariant, but then the figure is the schema turned, which is not the drawing the engineer drew, and a connector and a polar moment on one schema with different reference members would read different coordinates for the same markers. **Keeping only `axis`** and computing θ in Typst would have moved arithmetic into the display layer for no gain. `tests/test_schema_consumers.py` turns a whole detail by 30° and asserts that `local` does not move and `axis` does, which is the one test that could tell the two apart.
- *[in force]* **A source still carrying its own drawing is refused, not converted, settled (v0.74).** A 2.1 connection or a 1.1 connector is sent by name to `calc/tools/migrate_to_schemas.py`, which ran once on this project. The plan says one schema per source; writing two identical ones for `SD_01` and `SD_01-8mm` would have recreated, in the new folder, exactly the duplication this refactor removes. On this project four sources became **two** schemas, and `SD_01-8mm`'s second member, which had named `D001` — a position gone since v0.59 — now names `S001` through the schema its connector drew correctly.

The coordinates are the schema's now, so «no marker ticked» and «every marker deleted from the schema» are states of the **drawing**, not of the file — exactly as «no schema named» is — and the record says `calculated: false` with its reason. Refusing either would have made *Nouveau* open on an error, which v0.70 fixed, or stopped the whole export over a drawing only Woodcore can repair (the v0.33 rule). ⚠️ The line is unchanged where it matters: a *malformed* list — a marker named twice, an id that is not an id — still stops.
- *[in force]* **«Grille…» belongs to the schemas tab, settled (v0.74).** The plan asks that the grid write points *into the schema* from the connectors tab. It writes them from the «Schémas» tab instead, as a button in its *Connecteur* group. A second tab writing `details/<id>.json` could overwrite an unsaved draft of that very schema in the tab that owns it — silently, which is the one failure this project treats as worst. Chosen over teaching the connectors tab to check the schemas tab's dirty state, which would couple two tabs that otherwise know nothing of each other, for a feature that is one tab switch away. ⚠️ Accepted price: a grid is placed in one tab and ticked in another.
- *[in force]* **A new connector opens on the first schema there is, settled (v0.74).** *Nouveau* names it, ticks every one of its markers and names its first linear elements as the members. v0.70's rule is what makes this more than a convenience — «Nouveau» must open on something that calculates, not on a refusal — and with no schema named a fresh connector has no pattern at all. Where the project has no schema it still opens without one, and says so, which is honest: the detail is drawn first now, then calculated. A new *polar-moment* calculation does the same with the schema of the calculation selected, else the form's, else the first there is — it names no connector since v0.76.
- *[in force]* **The import graph is acyclic by construction, settled (v0.74).** `connector_svg` read `connection_svg` for colour constants, `connection_svg` reads `connection`, `connection` now reads `schema`, and `schema` reads `connector` for `TYPES` — a cycle, and it broke on **two of five import orders** the moment `connection` gained its edge. `connector_svg` takes the schema's constants now, and `connector` takes `connector_svg` and `schema` lazily, inside the functions that use them, which it already did for `dowel`. The one constant that pushed across the edge — `ENTITY_ID`, the id of an element or a marker — is restated in `connector.py` rather than imported, and `tests/test_constants.py` holds the two equal, as it already does for the kinds of fastener.

- *[in force]* **A cut piece keeps its position number, settled (v0.73).** An element split in two gives two elements carrying the same `position`, and only elements of the same `position` merge — a mismatch refusing **naming both numbers** (§3.3decies). It is legal downstream rather than merely convenient here: the position plan has allowed identical elements to share a number since v0.44, and what is said of a number is said once. Chosen over giving the second piece no number (which would make it an element the report cannot name) and over inventing one (which would put a number in the schema that `Positionen.xlsx` has never heard of, against §7's rule that the sheet is the one source of positions). Accepted price: two pieces of one beam are indistinguishable in the lists but for their ids, which is exactly what sharing a number means.
- *[in force]* **A modification is one step of «Annuler», and a refusal is none, settled (v0.73).** Everything a verb touches is bracketed in one `begin_change()`/`end_change()` pair, and the verbs that can refuse — *trancher*, *fusionner* — are tried on a **copy** first (§10quater). The first half is obvious once stated and was not free: six entries moved in six pairs would be six presses of *Annuler* to get back, which is not what the engineer did. The second half is the one worth recording: `begin_change()` is what *pushes* a step, so refusing after it leaves an undo that undoes nothing — and a cut that misses is the common case here, not the rare one. Chosen over taking the step back afterwards, which would need a second signal and an ordering nobody could check.
- *[in force]* **The selection is a focused entry and the rest, settled (v0.73).** `canvas.selection` is one `(collection, id)` and `canvas.extra` is the others, rather than one flat list (§10quater). Chosen over the flat list, which is the tidier data structure and the wrong model: the properties panel shows one entry's width, one list row scrolls itself into view, and a drag grabs one thing — all three would have had to invent a "first" anyway, and a Windows explorer selection has a focused item for the same reason. Accepted price: two fields to keep in step, which `select`, `select_many` and `selection_ids` are the only three places to do it in. ⚠️ A table holds one collection, so a selection spanning two of them shows in each tab as its own share; the canvas holds the whole, and the lists are a view of it.
- *[in force]* **«Trancher» takes its line from the selection, not from a third click, settled (v0.73).** The construction line to cut on is selected with the elements (Ctrl) and the button acts (§10quater). Chosen over a click that picks the line, which would make the gesture different for one element and for six, and over a combo of the schema's lines in the properties panel, which is a second way to say *this one* beside the selection that already exists. It is also what makes the line visibly part of what will be acted on, since every selected entry is outlined.
- *[in force]* **A detail is drawn once, in a module of its own, settled (v0.72).** *Moments polaires* and *Connecteurs* each carried a form of pieces and a table of fastener coordinates for the same physical geometry, which is the duplication §2 rule 1 forbids everywhere else; the «Schémas» module is the one place a detail is drawn (§3.3decies). Chosen over the two alternatives. Giving one of the two tabs the drawing and having the other read it would have made one of them the owner of something neither owns — a detail belongs to the connection, not to a calculation of it — and would have left the drawing unable to grow the construction lines, centres, areas and dimensions a detail actually needs, both tabs' forms being two-column forms. And extending both forms in step is the duplication itself, one version later. ⚠️ Accepted price at the time, and paid off since: **nothing consumed a schema in v0.72 or v0.73**, the two tabs carrying their own geometry beside it. v0.74 retired those forms (the decisions below).
- *[in force]* **The source folder is `details/`, not `schemas/`, settled (v0.72).** The plan this module was built from names the folder after the module, `schemas/<id>.json`; `000-Python/schemas/` has held every `*.schema.json` contract since v0.1, this module's own two among them, and `source_paths()` globs `*.json` without asking what kind of file it found. Pointed at that folder it read `calculations.schema.json` as a detail and refused it for having no `schema_version` — which is how it was found, on the first export of the first real example. `details/` is the module's own word for what it draws («un schéma est un détail à l'échelle réelle»), so nothing else had to be renamed: the module, `show-schema`, `data/schemas.json` and the two contracts keep their names. Chosen over renaming the contracts' folder, which every schema reference in this file and in `validation.py` names, for a collision that costs one word to avoid. `tests/test_schema.py` holds it from both sides — the contracts may not parse as sources, and `SOURCES` may not be that folder.
- *[in force]* **A named point is an aid, not a fifth collection, settled (v0.72).** A point worth naming — a centre, a reference point — is an `aids` entry of kind `centre` or `point`, and a dimension's `{"point": id}` anchor names one (§3.3decies). Chosen over statics.py's own arrangement, a flat `points` collection every other entry references by id: there, every member *is* two point ids, so the collection earns itself; here an element carries its own polyline and a marker its own x/z, so a shared collection would exist only to be anchored to, and «point» is already one of the aid kinds the tool bar offers. ⚠️ Accepted price: a dimension anchored to a place of its own does not follow the element under it when that element moves, exactly as a static model's `{x, z}` anchor does not. The engineer anchors to an aid where he wants it to follow.
- *[in force]* **A schema is drawn at its real size, settled (v0.72).** Its coordinates are millimetres and are used as they stand, scaled to `PAPER_MM` only when the SVG's width and height are set — `connector_svg.section()`'s arrangement — and **not** renormalised to 1000 figure units the way a static model's is (§3.3octies). The two are different drawings: a static model is a scale-free sketch, where a 0.5 m bench and a 20 m frame must come out identically, and a detail is a detail, where 12 mm beside 140 mm is the whole information. It is also what will let a calculation place its own force arrows over a copy of this drawing with no conversion, which is the fifth part's whole premise. ⚠️ Accepted price: the canvas's zoom limits are millimetre limits and had to be set apart from the static models', which are metre limits.
- *[in force]* **A construction line is not exported unless it is asked for, settled (v0.72).** A point or a centre carries `shown: true` and reaches the report; a line carries `false` (§3.3decies). An aid exists to get the drawing right, and a checker reading the report has no use for the lines that got it there — but the engineer sometimes means one, so `shown` makes it a decision rather than a rule, and a shown line is drawn more heavily inside the module so it reads as deliberate. Chosen over dropping the lines at the export, which would make «shown» a lie, and over printing them all, which is the clutter.
- *[in force]* **The three ways to draw a rectangle are two constructions, settled (v0.72).** Two diagonal clicks are their own shape; three clicks and «one click + Tab» both end as an edge and a signed width off it, so both call `schema.rectangle_from_edge` (§10quater). The plan names three modes and a reader could reasonably have written three; what the third click and the typed width have in common is that each is only ever a perpendicular distance off the first two clicks, and writing that twice is how the two come to disagree. A test holds them equal to the digit, because the engineer would meet the disagreement as a rectangle that jumps when he switches mode.
- *[in force]* **One moisture class for the connection, not one per member, settled (v0.70).** The source carries `moisture_class` at its top; a member's `material` carries none, and a schema-1.0 file's are collapsed into the worst as it is read (§3.3nonies). Chosen with the engineer over keeping one per member, which is what v0.68 had. SIA 265 applies a **single** η_w to R_d,Verb (Tabelle 4), and `Verbindung.xlsm` states one class per sheet (`C13`, `C35`); one per member meant the calculation had to decide which governed, and it took the worst — a rule the norm states nowhere. Now the engineer states it, or leaves it to the members' positions, where the worst still governs but does so **visibly**, with the classes it saw written beside it. Accepted price: two pieces genuinely in two ambiences are entered as the worse of the two, which is what the calculation did anyway and now says out loud.
- *[in force]* **A value written over a reference stays written over, settled (v0.70).** `{"position": "T001", "grade": "GL32h"}` was a third shape beside «follow» and «stated here» -- since schema 2.0 (v0.74) the position is the element's and the override is `{"grade": "GL32h"}` alone: the position is kept, the grade wins, and the next *Importer Excel* does not undo it (§3.3nonies). The engineer asked for it in those words. Chosen over the two alternatives. Reverting at the next import loses work with no word said, which is the failure this project treats as the worst kind. And **clearing the position** on an override — the tidier record — throws away the one thing that makes the orange line under the field mean anything: it is what lets Woodcore go on saying «position T001 holds C24» and offer *Reprendre*, and it is the element a schema will bind the member to. ⚠️ The line is composed by the **window**, from structured fields on the record, and not translated from a German sentence: a sentence with an id inside it is not a glossary key, and the two provenance templates that tried to be one swallowed each other's text (§4bis's own warning about a hole at the end of a template).
- *[partly superseded in v0.118]* **`plates.thickness` is removed until the check that reads it exists, settled (v0.70).** *The check exists since 12.10.2026 and reads the connector's `plate.t` (see «The steel plate's bearing is SIA 263 Tabelle 16 (76)…»); a member's `plates.thickness` stays refused.* It was in the source from v0.68 and nothing ever read it: it belongs to the plate's Lochleibungswiderstand (SIA 263 6.1.4), which is not built (§14). A field nothing reads is a field that ages — it acquires values nobody checks and a meaning nobody remembers — so it is gone, and the section figure draws the plate at the **slot width** instead, which is the one thickness the source does carry and the classeur's own `t`. Chosen over leaving it disabled with a tooltip, which is the same field with a longer explanation.
- *[in force]* **The wheel never changes a value, and it is stopped in one place, settled (v0.69).** One event filter on the *application* (`calc/tools/nowheel.py`), installed in `main()` before any window exists (§10quater). Asked for by the engineer in one word — «partout» — after a wheel meant to scroll a properties panel moved a load's angle by five degrees without saying so.
