Chosen over the two alternatives. **A subclass per widget** would have to reach 28 spin boxes, 25 combo boxes and the style sliders across four modules, plus the spin boxes a delegate builds for a table cell, which are written down nowhere — and a widget added next year would silently be uncovered. **`setFocusPolicy(Qt.StrongFocus)`**, the usual advice, does not do what it is said to do: Qt changes a spin box on a wheel whether or not it has the focus, so it fixes nothing. ⚠️ Accepted price, and it is deliberate rather than incidental: the event is eaten **and re-sent to the enclosing scroll area's viewport**, because eating it alone would leave the wheel doing nothing at all over a field, which is worse than the defect. ⚠️ A `QScrollBar` **is** a `QAbstractSlider` and is the one exclusion; a test holds it, since the naive rule gets exactly that case wrong and would disable the scrolling the guard exists to protect.
- *[in force]* **A folder is a field, not a directory, settled (v0.69).** Each source carries an optional `group` string and the left pane derives its folders from the values in use (`calc/tools/grouped_list.py`, §10quater). Asked for by the engineer for the four editor tabs, with drag and drop and the Ctrl/Shift selection of the explorer — and his own «Identité» box already named the field, «Groupe». Chosen over **real subdirectories**, which would make `connectors/Halle/SD_01.json` either an id of `Halle/SD_01` or a file whose name no longer says where it is: the rule that *the id is the file name* (§3.3quinquies) is what every `show-*` call and every cross-reference rests on, and a tidier listing is not worth touching it. As a field it is also **additive**: absent, empty or whitespace all read as the root, so no `schema_version` was bumped and no file needed migrating — a bump that forces no migration and means nothing is churn. ⚠️ Accepted price, two of them. **An empty folder cannot live in the project** — no source names it — so the set of them is a view preference in `settings.json`, and it does not travel to another machine with the repository. And **a drop is not Qt's own move**: the widget computes the target, emits `moved`, and the tab writes, exports and rebuilds, so a drop whose export fails leaves nothing half-moved. ⚠️ *Moments polaires* showed the folders and took no drop until v0.75, its left pane being a tree of connectors with their calculations beneath; since v0.75 it lists its calculations in folders of their own `group` and takes a drop like every other editor tab.
- *[in force]* **«Supprimer» archives into a directory, settled (end of v0.77).** A deleted source is moved into `archives/` beside the sources, its `id` removed (`source_archive.py`, §10quater), where a list folder stays a field. The two are not in tension: a folder only files a source, which a field does without touching the rule that the id is the file name; an archived source has to **leave the export and free its ID**, which a field could only do if every exporter and every «ID taken» check learnt to skip it, and a subfolder no `*.json` glob reaches does by construction. Chosen over deleting (the engineer's request, and a click nobody can take back) and over a `deleted` flag (the same readers taught to skip it, one of them forgotten). Accepted price: archives accumulate until deleted for good, and a restored source takes a new ID from its name — the Typst calls that named the old one stay broken until they are edited, which the confirmation says before archiving.
- *[in force]* **Every export is archived by one script, called identically by all three routes, settled (v0.71).** `calc/tools/archive_export.py` copies the PDF and a filtered snapshot of `000-TypstRapport/` into `000-Archives/` on every successful compile (§11, §12). Chosen over the three alternatives weighed. Versioning every export in Git, which is the very reason `000-Archives/` is itself gitignored (§13's own standing argument for Git as the traceability record, §1bis, would otherwise be duplicated by a second one nobody asked for). Archiving only the PDF, which cannot be reopened in Woodcore on the state that produced it — the whole point, since a PDF's own name never says which level or language it was compiled at, which is why the manifest (`archive-info.json`) records the `--input` flags verbatim rather than guessing them back from the number. And asking the engineer to confirm each time, which a convenience side-effect should not get to interrupt a build that has already succeeded. Accepted price, two of them: `000-Archives/` grows without bound, since nothing here prunes an old archive; and the snapshot excludes `000-ExternalFiles/` (~900 MB, never read by any module of `calc/`, §11bis), `examples/` and `out/` (documentation cache and build output, neither specific to one export) — so *rebuilding Woodcore identically* means the calculation state and the engine's own code at that moment, not a copy of everything that happens to sit under `000-TypstRapport/`.
- *[in force]* **A connector carries its own calculation, and leaves Excel, settled (v0.68), reversing v0.33.** A connector is a file of its own, `connectors/<id>.json`, written by Woodcore and read by `domains/connector.py` (§3.3nonies, §7bis). v0.33 put the connectors in `Positionen.xlsx` because a connector was a *position on the plan*, described like an element and entered like one; that argument was right and is now outweighed by a fact it could not have known. A connector's resistance needs the two members' thicknesses, their grain angles, their predrilling and the fasteners' coordinates -- none of which is a column, and all of which Woodcore already draws for a polar moment. Splitting one connector between a row in Excel and a drawing in the window would have been two sources for one thing (§2 rule 1). Chosen over the two alternatives: keeping the row and adding the calculation beside it (the split just described), and keeping the calculation in the workbook and importing its results (a second implementation of SIA 265, which is what v0.17 refused for snow). Accepted price: the engineer deletes four sheets by hand -- nothing here writes to the workbook -- so the export warns on every run until `Positions Connectors`, `data_connector`, `data_assembly` and `data_steel` are gone; and the ten descriptive keys had to be exported unchanged, which they were, so that `show-connector` and the reference workbook did not move.
- *[in force]* **One connector is one file is one calculation, settled (v0.68).** No variant beneath a connector, unlike a polar-moment calculation: to compare two layouts, duplicate the connector. Chosen by the engineer over the `<connector>-<variant>` arrangement the connections use, on the ground that a connector *is* the thing the report names -- `SD_01`, `SD_02` is how the sheet already numbered them -- and a second level would be a second id to keep in step. Accepted price: two layouts of one physical fastener are two connectors, and nothing says they are related.
- *[in force]* **k_beta from Anhang A, for every configuration, settled (v0.68).** SIA 265 6.2.1.1 offers Tabellen 45 and 46 as the more accurate alternative to the simplified Tabelle 23, and the engineer chose it. It is also what his own workbook computes, so the change is one of completeness rather than of method: the four configurations the norm gives a row for are implemented from the general form, and the «Für beta_f = 1» short form is not transcribed at all, being that form evaluated -- which a test asserts, and which is what would have caught the short form being mistaken for the steel row. ⚠️ A configuration the norm does **not** cover -- `Holz-Holz mehrschnittig`, a single one-sided plate, `Holz-Beton` -- is refused **naming 6.1.1.11**, which sends it to testing. Chosen over letting it through on the nearest row, which is an equation applied outside its domain without saying so.
- *[in force]* **A member's timber is a reference, resolved afresh at every export, settled (v0.68).** `{"position": "T001"}` reads that element's strength class and moisture class at every export; `{"grade": …, "moisture_class": …}` states them in the file (§3.3nonies). ⚠️ The shapes changed since, the decision did not: the moisture class became the connection's in schema 1.1, and in schema 2.0 (v0.74) the position is the one the member's schema element names, `material` being `null` (follow) or `{"grade": …}` (override). The engineer asked for it in those words: *«c'est le bouton Importer Excel déjà disponible»* -- there is to be no «Reprendre» button, because a value copied once is a value that goes stale without saying so. This is the static model's magnitude pattern (§3.3octies) applied to a position instead of a var, and it brings that pattern's rule with it: a reference that no longer resolves is a **warning**, never a stop. Accepted price, and it is real: the two positions of this project carry `moisture_class = 2`, so a connector taking its timber from them gives eta_w = 0,80 and **not** the resistance the workbook prints, which assumes class 1. That is the reference working, not a discrepancy.
- *[in force]* **A calculation that refuses does not stop the export, settled (v0.68).** `check_source` refuses a malformed **source** and stops, because that is a defect in the data (§3.6); a calculation refusing -- hardwood SIA 265 Tabelle 22 gives no row for -- means *the norm has no value for what was asked*, and that is a sentence to print rather than a reason to stop every other connector's export. The connector is exported with `calculated: false` and its reason, and the report refuses to print a resistance nobody produced. It is the v0.33 rule extended one step, and the line between the two halves is worth the sentence it takes to state.
- *[in force]* **One JSON file per position, settled.** Projects up to ~200 positions -> one file per position (`data/positions/<id>.json`) stays perfectly workable and is preferable to a single monolithic file: clear per-position Git diffs, isolated recalculation/validation possible, no giant file. Only worth reconsidering if a future project far exceeds that volume.
- *[in force]* **No load-to-position link of any kind, settled (v0.10 + v0.11).** v0.10 removed the generic, Excel-driven assignment layer (`data/assignments.json`, its schema, `loads/assignment.py`, the influence-width concept) and replaced it with per-position hand-wiring in Python (`export_all.py`'s `POSITION_INPUTS`); v0.11 removed that too (§8). A position is now exactly its five `Positions` columns plus `"domains": {}` — no span, no influence width, no load id, no derived line load. Accepted price: the ELU/bending worked example no longer appears in the report at all until a replacement mechanism is chosen (§14); the domain modules that computed it are kept, uncalled, rather than deleted.
- *[in force]* **A load is a domain whose instances are Excel sheets, settled (v0.18).** `data/loads.json`'s flat registry of load ids is gone: a load is a *domain* (`snow-load`, `dead-load`) with one *instance* per sheet carrying its prefix (§3.3). Chosen over inventing an id per case because the workbook already names them — its `Objekt` sheet tells the engineer to copy sheets and that the component name comes from the sheet name — so any separate id would be a second name for the same thing, kept in sync by hand. Two consequences follow, both wanted: copying a sheet adds a case to the export with no code edit (and to the report where `Sections/004` names it — v0.79, B4), and a sheet that produces two design loads (`SL-Dach2`'s q_(k,11) and q_(k,12)) is expressed as a `results` **list**, not forced into one value. Accepted price: a sheet renamed out of its prefix silently disappears from the report rather than erroring — nothing can tell an intentional rename from a deletion — so a domain that finds no sheet at all warns loudly.
- *[in force]* **`show-calc` renders a whole calculation, settled (v0.18).** It takes no step name: level 1 gives the results, level 2 the inputs and every step's formula and value, level 3 the substitution under each formula and the source table — always ending on the result. Chosen over the per-step call it replaced, which made `Sections/004` restate in nine hand-ordered calls a sequence the source sheet already fixes; the order is data now, not report code. `show-input` remains for pulling a single intermediate value into prose. Accepted price: a report cannot reorder or omit a step, only choose a level — if that is ever needed, it is a reason to change the data, not the display.
- *[in force]* **A calculation is printed in letters and then in numbers, settled (v0.20).** Every step renders as its formula and, under it, the same formula with its operands replaced by their values, the answer bold (§10bis). The substitution is *generated* from the step's `uses`, never authored beside the formula, because a hand-typed one is a second place for a number to live and the one place §2 rule 1 exists to prevent. `show-calc` walks the vars in data order rather than printing every input and then every step, which is what lets the same function produce a client report, an engineer report and a checking report from one `level` argument: the report's order and its level split are both decided in the domain module, where the calculation is. Accepted price, two of them. A substitution is string replacement on Typst math source, so a symbol that is a prefix of another is handled (longest first) but a formula whose symbols are not the operands' `symbol` fields will silently substitute nothing — it then falls back to `symbol = result` rather than printing the letters line twice. And a formula stating a bound rather than an equality cannot be closed with `= result` at all, so it keeps the older `mit x = ...` note, trailing the formula on the same row; that is detected from the formula (`is-bounded`), not declared in the data.
- *[partly superseded in v0.117]* **Snow is calculated in Excel, not in Python, settled (v0.17).** *A snow entered in Woodcore is computed in Python since issue #36 (11.10.2026, «The dead load, the snow and the wind are computed in Woodcore…» below); the `SL-*` sheets are still read so.* The `SL-*` sheets of `Belastungsannahmen.xlsm` own the snow calculation; `calc/domains/snow.py` reads its cells and contains no arithmetic (§3.3bis). Chosen over keeping a parallel Python implementation fed by an Excel-entered `s_k`/`mu`/`ce` — two implementations of one norm clause is exactly the duplication §2 rule 1 exists to prevent, and the sheet is the one the engineer actually fills in and checks. Accepted price: the exporter is coupled to cell addresses, so it fails loudly (naming the cell) rather than guessing when the sheet is reorganized, and openpyxl can only read values Excel has cached — an unsaved workbook exports nothing. This does not generalize to every domain by itself: `bending.py`/`combinations.py` remain Python, and which side owns a given calculation stays a per-domain decision, recorded in each var's `source`.
- *[in force]* **Permanent loads are calculated in Python, settled (v0.18).** The `EL+AL-*` sheets hold the layer build-up the engineer enters; `calc/domains/dead_load.py` computes every load from those raw entries and reads no formula cell (§3.3ter). The mirror image of the snow decision, and for the same reason applied to a different sheet: there, the sheet *is* the norm clause and duplicating it in Python would be the duplication; here the sheet is a data-entry table whose arithmetic is a sum, and doing it in Python buys a material lookup that is keyed correctly (`(Ebene, Material)`, not the first name match) and immunity to the broken external lookup on a copied sheet. Which side owns a calculation is therefore **not** a project-wide rule: it is decided per domain, and `engine` on the domain plus `source` on every var record which was chosen.
- *[partly superseded in v0.117]* **Wind is calculated in Excel, and its answer is a table, settled (v0.25).** *A wind entered in Woodcore is computed in Python since issue #37 (11.10.2026, below), its answer still a table; the `WL-*` sheets are still read so.* The `WL-*` sheets own the wind calculation and `calc/domains/wind.py` only reads them (§3.3quater) — the snow decision applied unchanged to a second sheet that is itself the norm clause. What is new is the shape of the answer: six global wind forces, printed as a table under one formula in letters, rather than one number under a substituted one. Chosen over the two alternatives, both of which the data would have had to lie for — a single "governing" q_k, which does not exist (b·h, d·h and d·b are three faces, not three candidates), and six steps with six substituted formulas, which is the same table set out in twelve lines. Accepted price: four optional fields on `table` (`level`, `place`, `formula`, and `value` columns), each of which says something the display cannot know — when a table is the answer rather than the working, where it therefore belongs, which formula it evaluates, and which var each cell shows.
- *[in force]* **The local wind forces are enveloped in Python, settled (v0.29).** The report prints, per surface and per opening case, the largest and smallest `q_p · (c_pe − c_pi)` over every wind direction (§3.3quater); the sheet combines the coefficients for one selected direction and opening only. Computing the envelope in `wind.py`, from the sheet's own coefficient block and with the sheet's own formula, was chosen over the two alternatives: an envelope block added to the workbook (the right home for it in the long run, but nothing here writes to the engineer's workbook, and every copied WL sheet would need it), and printing the sheet's single selection (a report whose numbers change with whichever case was last looked at in Excel). Accepted price: an `excel`-engine domain now carries one Python-computed table. It is bounded twice — the arithmetic is one line, checked against the sheet's own block wherever that shows a selection, and the table's `source` says where its numbers come from — and the cells stay plain numbers rather than eighty vars nobody quotes one at a time.
- *[in force]* **The load workbooks are found by name, settled (v0.26).** Every `.xlsm`/`.xlsx` directly in the project folder whose name contains `Belastungsannahmen` is read, not one fixed `Belastungsannahmen.xlsm` (§3.3). The sheet decision of v0.18 applied one level up, for the same reason: an office names its files (`02_Belastungsannahmen.xlsm`), and a fixed file name in the code would be a second name for that file, kept in step by hand. Chosen over the two alternatives: naming the file in a setting (one more thing to keep in sync, and still only one file), and searching subfolders (an archived or superseded copy would silently add its cases to the report). The instance id stays the bare sheet name rather than being qualified by its workbook — `show-calc("snow-load", "SL")`, not `"02_Belastungsannahmen.xlsm/SL"` — since the sheet name is the case's *Bauteilname* and what the report heads it with. Accepted price, two of them: a sheet name must be unique across all the load workbooks, which is refused loudly when it is not; and **every** matching file is read, so a stray copy left in the project folder (`Belastungsannahmen - Kopie.xlsm`) adds its cases too — which is why each export prints the files it took.
- *[in force]* **A norm table is a domain whose instances are its rows, settled (v0.49).** The Nutzlasten of SIA 261's Tabelle 8 are exported as the domain `live-load`, one instance per category, with `ENGINE = "norm"` and no sheet prefix (§3.3septies). Chosen over the two alternatives the engineer and I weighed. A sheet in the Belastungsannahmen workbook, per use zone, would have made every project retype a table the norm already fixes, and made a copied sheet a second source for it. And a table hard-coded in `lib/calc-engine.typ` would have put norm values in the display layer, where nothing validates them and no `source` records their edition. Accepted price: the twelve categories are all exported, whether the project uses one or four, and each carries its own copy of the table with its own row highlighted — the JSON is generated, so the duplication costs bytes and not a second place to edit. A third engine value came with it, checked on the domain, and `workbook`/`sheet` became facultative on an instance, since a norm row has neither.
- *[in force]* **The live load is printed by a command of its own, settled (v0.49).** `show-live-load(category)` lays out the category, what it is and its two values on one line, and sets Tabelle 8 above it at level 3 (§3.3septies). Chosen by the engineer over the generic `show-calc`, which renders the same record and still does: a lookup is not a calculation, and its line reads *Nutzungskategorie A1, Wohnflächen (…): q_k = …* rather than `Kategorie = A1; Nutzfläche = …`. The precedent is `show-polar-moment`, a command of its own on a record `show-calc` also reads. Accepted price: two ways to print one record, as for a connection. Everything either of them prints comes from the data, so they cannot say different things — and the one thing the dedicated command adds, the notes of Tabelle 8 as a single footnote, is a rule (say a note once) rather than a wording.
- *[in force]* **Category E is refused by the report, settled (v0.49).** SIA 261 8.2.2 gives no values for Lager- und Fabrikationsflächen: the project fixes them. Its instance therefore carries no `q_k` and no `Q_k`, and `show-live-load("E")` stops with the norm's own sentence (§3.3septies). Chosen by the engineer over printing a placeholder, which a reader could take for a value, and over taking values in the call, which would put an untraced number in a domain whose whole point is that its numbers are the norm's. Woodcore offers no `show-live-load` call for E, so the window never copies a call the report refuses. Accepted price: a project with a Lagerfläche states its values in the report itself until they have somewhere to be entered (§14).
- *[in force]* **A load's action is declared in the data, settled (v0.28).** Whether a load is permanent or variable is a constant of its domain module (`ACTION`), exported on the domain in `data/calculations.json` and validated (§3.3). Chosen over a mapping kept in Woodcore, the only reader today: that would be a second place to add a domain, updated by hand, and `combinations.py` (§3.5) needs the same classification to pick its factors — one declaration serves both. Accepted price: a required field on the domain, so `schema_version` went to 1.2.
- *[in force]* **Woodcore reads the export, not the workbooks, settled (v0.28).** The window (§10quater) shows `data/calculations.json` and builds its calls with `build_reference.py`'s `call_*` functions, never reading Excel itself. Reading the workbooks directly would show values nobody has validated, and calls that might not compile; this way what it lists is exactly what Typst will find. Its one action on the project is to rerun `export_all.py` on request, and it warns when a workbook is newer than the export. Three interface languages, the data's labels staying German.
- *[in force]* **Woodcore compiles the report itself, by calling Typst directly, settled (v0.62).** *Exporter PDF* runs `calc/export_all.py` and then `typst compile`, each in its own process, rather than shelling out to `build.ps1` or to `make` (§10quater). Asked for by the engineer, who wanted the window to export as well as import. Chosen over the two alternatives. `build.ps1` is the reference route on this machine (§12), but calling it would run the export a second time — the window has just run it — so `-SkipData` would have to be passed to the very script the window is deferring to, and PowerShell would stand between the window and processes it already starts and reads the exit codes of itself. `make` is not installed on this machine at all, which is why `build.ps1` exists. So the window builds the argument list `build.ps1` builds, names the same input and the same output, and takes `typst` off PATH as the script does. Accepted price: a second place where the compile command is spelled out. It is bounded by spelling exactly what the script spells — `$levelArgs`/`$langArgs` in `pdf_input_args()`, `$Main`/`$Report` as `MAIN_TYP`/`REPORT_PDF` — but a flag added to `build.ps1` does not reach the window by itself. And the window now writes the deliverable, where before it wrote only `data/`: deliberately the same file, since §12's whole point is that every route names one target.
- *[in force]* **The export menu is a set of checks, not a list of commands, settled (v0.62).** The entries of *Exporter PDF* are checkable and exclusive within their group, and the button is what compiles (§10quater). The engineer chose it over the alternative put to him, where each entry would have compiled at once with that one setting: that one cannot express `-Uniform 2 -Language fr`, a combination `build.ps1` documents, unless the menu carries the cross product of level and language. A dialog with fields was the third option and was refused — the window already has a menu, and a dialog puts a second window between a click and a PDF. Accepted price: checking an entry closes the menu and does nothing, which has to be learned once; the tooltip is what makes the state visible, and it says nothing while there is nothing to say. ⚠️ `-AllLevels`, `report-N` alone and `-SkipData` were offered and left out, so the menu changes what the one PDF contains and never which files are written: every export from the window re-exports the data first and writes `Statischer Rapport.pdf`, exactly as `.\build.ps1` with no argument does. The day the three variants in `out/` are wanted from the window, they are three more entries — and a decision about what *opens* afterwards, which the single deliverable makes trivial today.
- *[in force]* **Woodcore is a PySide6 window, settled (v0.30), reversing v0.28's tkinter.** Asked for by the engineer, together with an icon in the title bar and on the taskbar. Qt gives the native Windows 11 look, dark mode included, and scales each screen itself where the tkinter window scaled its own pixel sizes. v0.28 chose tkinter for the longevity reason of §1bis, and that reason carries less weight for this window than for the pipeline: nothing in the report depends on it. It only reads `data/` and copies text, the report builds without it, and `docs/typst-reference.xlsx` lists the same calls (§10ter). Accepted price: a dependency outside the standard library. It was already installed here (6.11.2), and a missing one is reported in a message box rather than as a window that silently never opens. The window is also slower to open: in the measurements here it was painted after 7–8 s against 5 s for tkinter, 3.5 s of both being `build_reference`'s import of openpyxl.
- *[in force]* **A connection is defined in Woodcore and saved as a source file, settled (v0.31).** The polar moments tab writes `connections/<id>.json` beside `calc/`, one file per connection, and nothing else writes it (§3.3quinquies). Chosen over the two alternatives the engineer had: a sheet per connection in a workbook — coordinates typed into cells are what the tab replaces, with no figure to check them against while typing — and one file for every connection, where each save would touch every connection's lines in Git. Beside `calc/` rather than at the top of the project folder, because the engineer edits it through the window and never opens it: the top of the folder stays the office's (§11). The id is the file name, so it is kept to characters safe in both; a file whose id is not its name is refused. Accepted price: for the first time the window writes into the project, which it does only on *Enregistrer* and *Supprimer*, each asking first where something could be lost.
- *[in force]* **A connection is exported on its own, into its own file, settled (v0.31).** `data/connections.json` beside `calculations.json`, not a domain inside it. Saving a connection exports the connections at once, in the window, with the very `connection.export()` export_all.py runs — so `data/` is still only ever written by the pipeline's code, and the report sees the connection without the load workbooks, slow to read and sometimes blocking on a defect of their own, being read again. Inside `calculations.json` a partial export would have had to rewrite the loads' half from the file it replaces, and the loads' rules (every domain an `action`, every instance a workbook and a sheet) would have needed exceptions. Read under the domain `connection` all the same, so `show-calc`/`var-of`/`instance-ids` work on it unchanged.
- *[in force]* **The forces in the fasteners are calculated in Typst, settled (v0.31).** `show-polar-moment` (named `show-fastener-forces` until v0.32, `show-fastener-polar-moment` until v0.38) distributes N, V and M itself — the one calculation in `lib/calc-engine.typ` — because they are written in the report: until `combinations.py` exports design forces there is nowhere else for them to come from, and a force needed in Python would have had to be typed into a file the report cannot see. Bounded three ways: what it distributes over is data (the coordinates, the rotation centre, I_p, the directions, all calculated and validated in Python); a force can be passed as a var, unit checked, so the day combinations exist the call takes them without changing; and Woodcore's preview computes the same distribution in Python (`connection.fastener_forces`), the two checked equal fastener by fastener on an irregular layout with a fixed centre and arbitrary angles. Accepted price: those forces are not traced — no `source`, not in `data/`, not catalogued — which is §1's standing condition for a calculation done in Typst, and the reason the preview's numbers are never saved.
- *[in force]* **A comparison written by hand is compared by the engine, settled (v0.58).** `#test(left, op, right, unit:)` — `#verify` since v0.61, which keeps every rule below and reads its two sides out of the registry of variables (§10quinquies) — takes the two numbers, compares them itself, and prints the comparison double-ruled with a ✔ or a ✘ (§10bis). The engineer asked for it as «une vérification python», and the first thing to settle was that there is no such thing: **Typst cannot call Python at compile time** — no shell, no FFI, only WASM plugins — so a comparison written in the report is a comparison performed in the report. Chosen over the two alternatives. Marking a verdict the caller states, which is what the `verdict` command it replaces did, leaves the one thing that can be wrong — whether it holds — to the eye that already wrote the numbers. And routing it through a Python domain, where a `status` is data and `show-check` prints it, is the traced answer and the right one, but it is blocked on §14 and could not carry these numbers anyway: the Sherpa capacities of `Sections/005` are read off a manufacturer's table and belong to no workbook. Accepted price, and it is the same one §1 has always named: the verdict is untraced. It is not in `data/`, carries no `source`, is catalogued by no reference workbook, and prints no citation — the surrounding prose must, if it matters, the trade `inline-value` already makes. What it does buy is narrow and worth stating: the mark cannot contradict the numbers printed beside it, and neither number is retyped. On its first call site both had been — `#verdict[$F_"Ed" = 2.037 "kN" ...$]` restated by hand the `F-Ed` and `R-d` computed six lines above it (§2 rule 1). Two further rules follow from making it compare rather than assert: content is **refused** rather than parsed, since `#fmt(x)` is a `context` block whose fields are empty and whose output no code can read back, so `$#fmt(F-Ed) "kN"$` carries no number to recover; and two sides whose units disagree stop the compile, a guarantee neither the `verdict` nor the hand-written equation before it ever gave.
- *[in force]* **The mark is the report's own font, settled (v0.58).** The **heavy** glyphs, U+2714 ✔ and U+2718 ✘, never the light ✓ and ✗. Measured against Libertinus Serif's own notdef width with `fallback: false`: the heavy pair are real glyphs there (6.5 and 6.84 pt against a 5.5 pt notdef) and the light pair are not (5.5 pt, both). Libertinus Serif is the font Typst bundles and this report is set in, so the heavy pair cannot fail anywhere; the light pair rendered on the machine this was written on only by falling back to Segoe UI Symbol — a sans-serif tick in a serif report, and an empty box on any machine without it. ⚠️ Typst 0.14 warns about **neither**, so a tofu would have shipped silently; the choice is made on the measurement and not on how it looked. For the same reason the mark is never set bold: Libertinus Serif **Bold** covers neither pair. Colour and size carry it instead.
- *[superseded in v0.74]* **A connection is written in the frame of a reference member, settled (v0.32), replacing v0.31's page frame.** x along the grain of a member the engineer picks, z a quarter clockwise from it (down when x points right), every angle counted from x towards z, M positive from z towards x (§3.3quinquies). Asked for by the engineer: a connection's numbers are read against a member's grain — the force-grain angle is what the embedment strength will be read with (§14) — which a frame fixed to the page (x right, y up) says nothing about, and z down is the convention of the frame programs the office models in (a member's local axes in RFEM). M was left anticlockwise-positive, against the clockwise sense the angles now take, so that a moment already written in the report for v0.31 keeps its physical sense; the price is a moment term whose two signs differ (F_x gains +M·Δz/I_p, F_z −M·Δx/I_p), stated in the footnote under the formula. A v0.31 file was converted as it was read rather than refused: the conversion is exact, and the forces printed for it do not change. ⚠️ Both halves were superseded in v0.74 (above): the frame is the detail schema's, `reference` only names the Bezugselement, and a 1.0, 2.0 or 2.1 source is refused by name and converted once by `calc/tools/migrate_to_schemas.py`.
- *[in force]* **The force arrows are drawn by the report, into the exported figure, settled (v0.32).** `connection_svg.py` draws the connection once and leaves a marker; `show-polar-moment` inserts every fastener's N/n, V/n and F_i at the marker before setting the SVG (§3.3quinquies). Chosen over the two alternatives. Python cannot draw them, since N, V and M are the report's and never reach it. And Typst drawing the whole figure would make two drawings of one connection, Woodcore's and the report's, free to drift apart. Accepted price: the arrows themselves are drawn twice, by `connection_svg.force_arrows` for Woodcore's preview and by `fastener-arrows` in `lib/calc-engine.typ`, kept rule for rule alike and compared on the same forces. One scale for every arrow was the engineer's request, and after one detour it holds without exception (v0.50). v0.32 held N/n and V/n to a minimum length, so that they would not vanish under a moment, and marked them in the legend as enlarged. What that cost became visible once the moment's own force was drawn on each fastener (v0.50): the engineer added the blue, the red and the green by eye and did not get the yellow. On the project's figure the blue was 86 times its true length. So the minimum is gone, and with it the two alternatives that share its fault: leaving a small share out breaks the same addition, by the length left out, and two scales would make F and N/n incomparable even without a moment. Accepted price: where a moment dwarfs them, N/n and V/n come out too small to read, which the legend's note says with their values.
- *[in force]* **Tables are centred in the space they have, settled (v0.32).** A table narrower than the calculation's column is centred in that column — the text width less the citation column — and one wider, or with a `wide` column, keeps the full width (§3.3). Asked for by the engineer for every table. Centring on the full text width would set a narrow table's middle off the formulas above it, which end where the citations begin. Accepted price: `render-table` measures its table inside `layout()`, where content breaks across pages only inside a breakable `block` and never inside a `box`; the long local wind table was checked to break with its header repeated, and the layer build-ups, which have a `wide` column, render as before. Asked for next, on the same grounds: tables at the text's size and with room around them. The larger text made the local wind table miss the rest of a page by a row, leaving its title and formula alone at the foot of the page. So a table shorter than a page is now kept whole, and moves to the next page with them. Accepted price: some white space at the foot of the pages such a table leaves. Two related guards came with it. The headings of `show-calc` and `show-polar-moment` are `sticky`, never left at the foot of a page. The connection's rotation-centre lines stay together.
- *[superseded in v0.68]* ~~**Connectors are entered in Excel, as a second sheet of positions, settled (v0.33).**~~ **Reversed in v0.68** (above): a connector is a file of its own, `connectors/<id>.json`, written by Woodcore; what follows is kept as the record of v0.33. A connector is a row of `Positions Connectors`, beside the elements' sheet, which the engineer renamed `Positions Element`; its type, kind of assembly and steel are picked from `data_*` lists, as an element's type is (§7bis). Asked for by the engineer. It was chosen over defining connectors in Woodcore, where the connections are: a connector is a position on the plan, like an element, and belongs where the positions are entered and where the future resistance calculation will read them. Accepted price: Woodcore cannot offer a connector added in Excel until the workbook is re-exported, which its banner now says (`Positionen.xlsx` newer than `data/connectors.json`). The workbook was given its new sheets once, by a script, with the engineer's agreement, the pipeline still never writing to it.
- *[superseded in v0.76]* ~~**A polar-moment calculation belongs to a connector and is named after it, settled (v0.33).**~~ **Reversed in v0.76** (above). A calculation's id is `<connector>-<variant>` (`SD001-V1`), and several may belong to one connector, to compare layouts (§3.3quinquies). The engineer chose this over a free id carrying a link: the id then says what it is a calculation of, in the report's calls as in the file list. Accepted price: moving a calculation to another connector renames it, so a call in `Sections/` using the old id must follow. Woodcore asks before renaming, as it already did.
- *[superseded in v0.76]* ~~**A calculation of no listed connector is warned about at export and refused by the report, settled (v0.33).**~~ **Reversed in v0.76** (above): there is no link left to be missing. Such a calculation — a v0.32 source, or one whose connector row was renamed or deleted — does not stop `export_all.py`. It stops `show-polar-moment` and every lookup of the domain `connection`, with the way to link it. Chosen over stopping the export, which would have been a deadlock: the connectors would not be exported either, and Woodcore, which offers only exported connectors, could not link the calculation. Chosen over printing it without its connector, which would put a calculation in the report that says nothing about what it concerns. Accepted price: the report does not build between renaming a connector in Excel and relinking its calculations — the project's A01, saved before v0.33, until the engineer links it.
- *[in force]* **A position plan is drawn in Woodcore and saved as a source file, settled (v0.34).** The «Plan de positions» tab writes `plans/<id>.json`, one file per plan, and exports it at once (§3.3sexies). This is the connections' arrangement, chosen for the same reasons (§13 above). The engineer asked for Woodcore to become an editor. The reference HTML editor in `000-positionsPlan` was not embedded in a web view: its code is a third party's, its model doubles Positionen.xlsx and assigns loads to elements (§8), and it would have brought Chromium into the window. What was taken from it is the STE symbology, reimplemented in `plan_svg.py`.
- *[superseded in v0.94]* **Positionen.xlsx stays the source of positions, and a plan only locates them, settled (v0.34).** An element's label is a position id; a label the sheet lacks is a warning, never an error, and Woodcore copies the rows to paste into the sheet rather than writing to it. Chosen by the engineer over two alternatives: a blocking check, which would stop the report until Excel is filled in, and the plan as the source of positions, which would have made §7's sheet a second source for its ids.
- *[in force]* **A plan draws only the sheet's positions, settled (v0.45), tightening v0.34 in Woodcore and not at the export.** The engineer asked for it: a position is entered in Excel first, then drawn. Woodcore draws nothing for a letter the sheet lacks, offers only the sheet's numbers, and refuses to save a plan carrying another (§3.3sexies). The export still only warns, as for a connection of no listed connector (v0.33, above), to avoid the same deadlock. A number the sheet lost after it was drawn would otherwise stop the positions' export as well, and Woodcore, which offers only exported positions, could not give the element its new number. Accepted price: a plan carrying such a number cannot be saved, whatever else was changed, until that number is replaced. It can still be viewed and corrected, since Woodcore opens a source without checking it.
- *[in force]* **A plan is two layers in Typst, the architect's page and an SVG of the symbols, settled (v0.34).** Typst 0.14 sets a PDF page as a vector image, so the architect's plan stays exactly as drawn and the symbols' SVG stays small and readable in a diff. A single SVG was the alternative. pdftocairo, from TeX Live, can convert the page, but gave 2.6 MB for the Pavillon's (against 0.4 MB for its three-page PDF), would add that much to Git with every revision of the architect's plan, and needs TeX Live on every machine. The page is copied under `data/plans/`, reduced to itself by pypdf, because Typst reads nothing outside `000-TypstRapport/` and because the copy is then versioned with the report. Widening `--root` to the project folder would have left the report depending on an unversioned folder. Accepted price: pypdf, a dependency outside the standard library, imported only when a plan has a PDF background.
- *[in force]* **Woodcore renders the part of a plan in view first, and keeps every render, settled (v0.48).** The engineer found changing plan slow, architects' PDFs being heavy. What costs is not the file — loading the 6.4 MB plan set takes 0.07 s — but rasterizing a dense sheet, and the time grows faster than the pixels (§10quater). So the view is rendered before the whole page, the whole page at 1200 px rather than 2500, and every render is kept, in memory and on disk. Rendering only the crop and never the whole page was the alternative: the whole page is what shows while the view moves and when it zooms out, and at 1200 px it costs about a second, once per page and session. The disk cache was the engineer's choice over memory alone, which loses everything at each launch; it lives in the user's local profile, outside the project — in the project it would be synced by NextCloud and versioned with the report, both for files that a render reproduces. Accepted price: renders on disk that a PDF replaced makes stale, which the key (the file's size and the time it was written) turns into a miss rather than a wrong plan, and the folder grows to 300 MB before the least recently used go.
- *[in force]* **The highlight is drawn by Typst, and a detail keeps the plan's scale, settled (v0.34).** Which positions are highlighted is the call's to say, so Typst inserts their halos into the plan's SVG at a marker, as it inserts a connection's force arrows (§13 above). A detail is printed at the whole plan's scale rather than enlarged to a fixed width. The symbols are sized by the whole plan's frame, so enlarging a detail would enlarge its symbols with it, while redrawing them per detail would need an SVG per combination of positions. `zoom` enlarges a detail when asked to.
- *[in force]* **Every file imports the whole engine through `commands.typ`, settled (v0.40).** `000-TypstRapport/commands.typ` re-exports `lib/calc-engine.typ` with a wildcard, and every `.typ` of the report imports it with `*` (§10bis). Asked for by the engineer, who had to edit the import line of every section using a command each time one was added or renamed. Chosen over a list of the public functions in `commands.typ`: that would be one more list to keep up to date by hand, beside the `///` headers, and a name left off it would fail only where it is first used. Accepted price: Typst has no private bindings, so the engine's helpers (`render-table`, `var-row`, …) are in every file's scope and in the editor's autocomplete. That is harmless as long as none of them takes a Typst global's name, the rule §10bis states.
- *[in force]* **A static model is drawn in x/z, not x/y, settled (v0.51).** x to the right and **z down**, angles from x towards z, which is the connection module's frame (§3.3quinquies) and the one the office's frame programs use. Asked for by the engineer on first use, against the y-up frame the module was written with. It costs nothing and buys two things: the drawing needs no flip at all, so what is typed, what Woodcore shows and what the report prints are one set of numbers; and the two figures this engine draws now agree with each other. Accepted price: the sources written in the first hours had to be converted, which was a sign flip on every z and every offset.
- *[in force]* **A translational spring is a member, not an articulation, settled (v0.51).** `MEMBER_KINDS` is `bar`, `cable`, `spring`, each with its own drawing and a spring with its own stiffness; a release is free in moment, shear or normal force, and only a moment release may be a spring. Asked for by the engineer, who looked for the spring among the members. It is also the truer reading: a release frees a degree of freedom **at an end**, while a translational spring is a thing between two points with a length and a stiffness of its own. Accepted price: a release that carried a translational stiffness -- the form of the first hours -- keeps its degree of freedom and loses the stiffness, since turning it into a member would be a change to the structure, and that is the engineer's to make.
- *[in force]* **A static model is a drawing and not a calculation, settled (v0.51).** Woodcore's static models tab draws a system -- members, supports, releases, loads -- and computes no reaction, no N/V/M and no check (§3.3octies). That is the engineer's own boundary, stated in the request, and it is the one most likely to be pushed on later: a system that is drawn correctly looks as though it could be solved. Its record carries no `{vars, steps, checks}` shell for the same reason, so nothing addresses one with `show-calc` and nobody can mistake a figure for a traced calculation (§1). Accepted price: the reactions an engineer writes beside such a figure are typed into the report by hand, untraced, until `combinations.py` has a caller (§14).
- *[in force]* **A load's magnitude may name a variable of the export, settled (v0.51).** `{domain, instance, var}` in place of `{value, unit}`, resolved at every export against `data/calculations.json` (§3.3octies). Asked for by the engineer -- «également à partir d'une variable» -- and chosen over a Typst argument substituting the value at compile time, which was the other reading. Two reasons. A label's box is sized by its own text: the figure reserves the room, places the label clear of the arrows and sizes the frame around it, so a string substituted later would overlap what it was placed clear of. And the magnitude *can* exist in Python, unlike a connection's N, V and M, so a `values:` argument would be a third place for a number the data already holds, against §2 rule 1. Accepted price: a value only changes when the export is rerun, which is what Woodcore's *Importer Excel* is for.
- *[in force]* **The influence width is applied in Python, and it is the module's only arithmetic, settled (v0.51).** A load may carry b_e in metres; the magnitude is multiplied by it and one metre comes off the unit, kN/m² becoming kN/m (§3.3octies). Asked for by the engineer, whose actions are calculated per square metre while his systems carry per metre. Chosen over leaving the multiplication to the engineer, which would have put a hand-computed number on a figure whose whole point is that its numbers are the workbook's. It is bounded: the arithmetic is one line, the unit rule refuses anything that is not per metre or per square metre, and the export keeps the value before the width, the width and the product, so the chain can be read back. Accepted price: the one calculation in a module that says it does none -- which is why the figure prints b_e beside the load and its description underneath, rather than only the product.
- *[in force]* **A model's influence width passes over a load it cannot apply to; a load's own is still refused, settled (v0.51, second review).** The engineer asked for a b_e on the whole drawing, overridable per load. A width divides one metre off a unit, so it cannot apply to a load in kN — and refusing it there would make a single global b_e impossible on any model carrying one point load, which is most of them. The two are therefore read differently: a **model's** width is a default and passes silently over such a load, a **load's own** is a statement about that load and is refused by name. Chosen over the two alternatives: refusing the model's width as well, which defeats the feature, and applying it to a kN load anyway, which would print a number in a unit nothing produced. Accepted price: one rule that is not uniform, which is why `resolve` takes the fact as an argument (`width_optional`) rather than inferring it.
- *[in force]* **A static model's figure is normalised, not drawn in metres, settled (v0.51).** The model's longer side is drawn as 1000 figure units and every symbol size is a constant (§3.3octies). It came out of a defect: at a quarter of a user unit QtSvg drops a `<text>` while still painting its white halo, so a figure drawn in metres showed haloes and no labels. Normalising fixes that and buys the scale invariance for nothing -- a 2 m model and a 20 m one render to the same digits -- where sizing every literal against the extent would have had to be got right symbol by symbol. Accepted price: the record's coordinates are not metres, so the only thing that reads them is the halo Typst inserts into the same SVG; a member's real length is exported beside them, in metres.
- *[in force]* **A dimension is one collection with a `kind`, settled (v0.55).** The four tools -- horizontal, vertical, parallel and angle -- write into one `dimensions` collection with a `kind` discriminator, not into two collections (§3.3octies). Chosen over splitting the angle off: it is one word in the engineer's head, «une cotation», and one collection is one list tab, one schema block, one record array and one set of checks. Accepted price: three of the four fields mean something slightly different for an angle -- its ends are members and its offset is a radius -- which is said in the schema and checked by name rather than left to be discovered.
- *[in force]* **A dimension's end is an anchor, and its offset is measured from the middle, settled (v0.55).** `{"point": "P1"}` follows the point it names; the offset is one signed number in metres from the **middle of the two ends** (§3.3octies). Chosen over saving the third click as a point of its own, which is the obvious reading of «distance perpendiculaire aux 3 points» and the wrong one: a dimension whose line stays where it was clicked while the model moves under it is a dimension that lies, and the engineer moves points constantly. This is v0.42's `label_offset` rule applied to a second thing (§3.3sexies). Accepted price: a dimension cannot be put anywhere its two ends do not reach -- it is always square to them, which is what a dimension is.
- *[in force]* **The third click names the sector, settled (v0.57), replacing v0.55's sign.** An angle dimension carries a `bearing`, the direction of that click from the corner, and the arc is the sector it fell in — any of the four, at most 180 degrees (§3.3octies). v0.55 kept it to the sign of the radius and one bit cannot say which of four; worse, the two rays it measured between were the members' stored p1 → p2 directions, which are not rays of the corner, so two of the four sectors were unreachable whatever the sign. Chosen over storing the click itself, which a moved member would drag into another sector, and over always taking the smaller angle, which would make a reflex impossible to write — a negative radius still writes one. The engineer states the angle by pointing at it, which is what he was already trying to do.
- *[in force]* **A dimension measures and does not solve, settled (v0.55).** It is the v0.51 boundary applied to what the drawing now carries: a dimension states a distance or an angle that the geometry already has, and nothing here computes a reaction, a length that was not drawn, or a dimension chain that adds two of them up. The day a chain is wanted, it is a second collection of its own, not arithmetic hidden in this one.
- *[in force]* **The report's language is a setting of the document, settled (v0.52).** One command in `Statischer Rapport.typ`, `#set-report-language("fr")`, beside the base level and the decimals, with `--input lang=` to force it for one run (§4bis). Asked for by the engineer in that form. The alternative was to make it an argument of the export, so that Python could write the whole of `data/` in the target language — which would have made the *data* language-bound, needed a re-export to change language, given Woodcore a language to guess whenever it saves a connection or a model, and put a second declaration beside the document's. Accepted price: Python does not know the language, so anything Python has to *draw* to measure — a figure's frame — is produced once per language (below).
- *[in force]* **One glossary, and the German is its key, settled (v0.52).** `calc/i18n/strings.json`'s `terms` block keys the German the export already carries by itself, the way gettext keys by its source string (§4bis). Chosen over the two alternatives. Exporting three variants of every label (`"label": {"de": …, "fr": …, "en": …}`) would have widened `label`, `description`, `title`, `note`, `group_label` and `name` in six schema files, made the hand-rolled validator's error messages print a dict, and given Woodcore and `build_reference.py` a variant to pick — for text that is generated anyway. Exporting a key beside the German would have put the same wording in two files at once. As it is, **`data/calculations.json` did not change by one byte**, and the rule the glossary draws is the one the engineer stated: a string is the engine's word if the glossary carries it, and the engineer's if it does not. Accepted price: a German label renamed in a domain module without its glossary entry silently falls back to German in the other two languages. It degrades rather than breaks, the glossary is one file to look in, and the coverage can be re-checked by walking the export's text fields against `terms` (§14).
- *[in force]* **A sentence with a hole is matched, not exported with its parts, settled (v0.52).** The eighteen German sentences that interpolate a sheet's name, a chosen value or a figure's number are recognised by the literal segments around their holes and re-emitted in the target language with the captures put back by name (§4bis). Chosen over having Python export `description_key` + `description_args` for them: that is the textbook shape, and it would have meant editing the four load domains, two schemas and the validator for eighteen strings. Matching literal segments needs no regular expression, cannot be confused by a sheet's name, and fails safe — an unmatched sentence stays German. Accepted price: a template's German literal has to stay distinctive enough to be recognised, which is why a template's holes are surrounded by real words and never by punctuation alone.
- *[in force]* **A figure is drawn once per language, settled (v0.52).** `data/connections/<id>-fr.svg` and `data/statics/<id>-fr.svg` beside their German and English twins, the record carrying one file each — and, for a static model, one frame each, since `show-static-model` sizes the image from the record and `fit: "stretch"` would distort a figure whose frame did not match (§4bis). It follows from where the text is measured: a connection figure's frame is sized from its legend's five sentences and a static model's from its notes, both in Python, which does not know the report's language. Two alternatives were set aside. Carrying all three languages in one SVG, as marked groups Typst strips, would still have had to size the frame for the longest of the three and would have left two languages' text in every figure. Moving the legend and the notes out of the SVG into Typst, as v0.39 did for the plan legend, is the better end state but is a redesign of two renderers that agree rule for rule — and for a static model it would take the notes out of the drawing Woodcore shows. Accepted price: three files where there was one, identical when a figure has no engine words in it at all (this project's `Beispiel`), and a third of the export's figure work spent on languages a given report does not use.
- *[in force]* **Numbers do not follow the language, settled (v0.52).** The decimal separator is a point in German, French and English alike (§4bis), which is what `norms/sia261_tables.json` already said of its own transcription. Chosen by the engineer over a comma in French: it would have to be taught to `fmt-decimals` and to every substituted formula, where Typst's math mode reads a comma as punctuation and `f_(m,k)` already uses one — and the existing PDFs would all change.
- *[superseded in v0.54]* ~~**The standard headings of `Sections/` come from the glossary, the prose does not, settled (v0.52).**~~ **Reversed in v0.54** (below): the headings are the engineer's too. What survives of it is the half that was never in doubt — the prose of `Sections/` stays as the engineer writes it, in the language of the report being written, rather than going through a `#tr(de:, fr:, en:)` helper that would have made one set of section files serve all three languages. Accepted price, unchanged: a report written in two languages needs its prose written twice, or two sets of `Sections/`.
- *[in force]* **What the engineer writes by hand is never translated, settled (v0.54), reversing v0.52's choice of glossary headings.** The headings of `Sections/` and the document's title are his words and are written in the language he is writing the report in; the sixteen `section.*`/`report.title` keys are gone from the glossary (§4bis). His instruction, on seeing v0.52 in use: the rule is not "everything the engine prints" but "everything the engine composes", and a heading he typed is neither. It is also the simpler line to hold — the glossary's German keys mean a translation can only happen where a German string reaches the engine, and a heading never does. Accepted price: a heading that a future report wants in three languages has to be written three times, or the report kept to one language, which is what the surviving half of the v0.52 decision already says of the prose.
- *[in force]* **The document's language and the engine's language are two settings, in two files, settled (v0.54).** `config.typ` declares `doc-lang`/`doc-region`, which is what Typst itself works in — «Abbildung»/«Fig.», the outline's title, the hyphenation; `#set-report-language` stays in the entry file and governs only the glossary (§4bis). The engineer chose this over the two alternatives. Deriving Typst's language from the report language, which is what v0.52 did, ties two things that are not the same question: a report whose engine speaks French may be written in German, and v0.52 made «Abbildung» and the outline's title move with the calculation labels whether or not that was wanted. Deriving the report language from `config.typ` instead would have made `--input lang=fr` impossible, since a `set` rule is not a state a build flag can reach. Accepted price: a French report needs two edits rather than one, which is exactly the point — each setting answers a different question, and `config.typ` reading no state is what lets it stay the plain `set`/`show` file its own header promises.
- *[in force]* **A sentence in the data may carry math between dollars, settled (v0.54).** `render-prose` splits a printed sentence on `$` and evaluates the odd parts as Typst math, so a note reads `c_red` rather than `cred` (§4bis). Asked for by the engineer, who found `cred` in a table's legend. Chosen over the two alternatives. Giving a note a structured shape — a list of text and symbol pieces — would have widened `note`, `description` and `desc` in the schemas, the validator and Woodcore, for a handful of strings. And interpreting the whole sentence as markup would make an Excel description a source of Typst syntax, where an underscore or an asterisk the engineer typed would silently change the page. The dollar interprets nothing else, and an unpaired one leaves the sentence alone, so «5$ pro Stück» survives. Accepted price: one character of the data now has a meaning, which is why the German source and the glossary must carry the same spans — the German being the key, a mismatch shows up as an untranslated sentence rather than as a wrong one.
- *[in force]* **A calculation written by hand is computed by the engine, settled (v0.60).** `eq` takes the formula once, in math source, prints it in letters and in numbers, and **evaluates it** — units included (§10quinquies). Asked for by the engineer after v0.59's reading found three lines in `Sections/005` whose printed formula did not produce the number beside it. Chosen over the two alternatives. Leaving it to the eye is what had already failed, three times on one page. And putting every such calculation into a Python domain is the traced answer and still the right one where it is available — but it is not available here: a Sherpa capacity read off a manufacturer's table belongs to no workbook, and §14's first item is still open. Accepted price, unchanged from §1: the answer is untraced. What is bought is that the printed formula, the printed numbers and the printed answer are now one statement rather than three.
- *[in force]* **The units are an algebra, not a decoration, settled (v0.60).** A unit is parsed into a scale factor and a map of base dimensions; the formula's operations propagate them; the answer is converted into the declared unit or the compile stops — until v0.63, when an unreachable declared unit became a red line under an answer printed in the operands' own unit (below). The engineer asked for it explicitly. Chosen over printing the units and trusting them: `Sections/005` carried `M_"Ed" / a dot 10^6` and `dot 1000` precisely *because* nothing did the conversion, and a hand-written conversion factor is where a report goes wrong by a thousand. N is kept as a base rather than resolved to kg·m/s², because nothing in a structural report mixes the two and every message then reads in the units an engineer writes. ⚠️ Accepted limit, and it is stated in the code and held by a test: the algebra checks **dimensions**, never **sizes**. `1000` is a dimensionless factor, so `M / a dot 1000` is valid and comes to 2037 kN. What guards that is the substituted line, which prints the factor where a reader sees it.
- *[in force]* **A variable has one name, and it lives in a registry, settled (v0.61).** `#var("R_k", 7, "kN")` writes a variable down and prints it; a formula names the registry's keys, so there is no `where:` and no `#let` (§10quinquies). Chosen after the engineer used v0.60's `eq` and found it unreadable — a variable carried three names that had to agree and that nothing checked. ⚠️ The first thing settled was what is **not possible**: `#var(N, "N", 0, kN)` cannot create the binding `N`, because Typst gives a function no way to bind a name in its caller's scope, and `#eq(R-d, "R_d", k-dens dot eta-w)` cannot be a bare expression, because `dot` is math and an argument is evaluated before the call. Those two are closed, not open. Accepted price: a value lives in a state, so it cannot be read outside a `context` — which is why `show-polar-moment` learned to take a name, `N: "N_Ed"`, and why `#eq` prints instead of returning.
- *[in force]* **A named domain, not a scope that unwinds, settled (v0.61).** `#var-domain("S10 - 0.55 kN Ende")[ … ]` keeps one column's variables apart from the next *and leaves them readable further down the page*, through `#val(domain, key)` (§10quinquies). The engineer's own idea, and better than the `var-scope[...]` that was proposed: a scope that pops would have lost what the column wrote, and the reason he wanted the separation in the first place was to use both afterwards. A domain is a key in the registry, so nothing is ever popped but the current-domain marker. Accepted price: one more word overloaded — a *domain* here is a set of names in the report and not a calculation domain (§3.3), which the documentation says wherever it could mislead.
- *[in force]* **`verify` and not `eval`, settled (v0.61).** The engineer proposed `#eval(F-Ed <= R-d)`. `eval` is a **Typst global**, and `commands.typ` re-exports the engine at the wildcard, so the name would be masked in every section — the one rule §10bis puts on the engine's names. He chose `verify` from four. The relation itself is a raw block for the reason a formula is: `F-Ed <= R-d` written as an expression would be evaluated before the call, comparing two dictionaries and losing the structure to print. What it buys over `test`, which it replaces: neither number is retyped, the units come from the algebra rather than from a `unit:` written twice, and the printed sign is the one that actually holds.
- *[partly superseded in v0.63]* **The unit of a result need not be declared, settled (v0.61); *how* it is chosen, reversed in v0.63.** That a result comes out in the unit an engineer writes, without `unit:` on every line, is settled and unchanged — it is what the engineer asked to be rid of. What v0.61 chose, and v0.63 undid, is choosing it **on the value's size**: a list of candidates per dimension, the first landing between 0.1 and 1000 winning, so that a stress printed in N/mm² and a snow load in kN/m² though they share a dimension. See the v0.63 decision below for why that had to go and what it cost.
- *[in force]* **One unit per dimension, and `#unit` sets it, settled (v0.63).** A table keyed by the dimension, one entry each, no magnitude window (§10quinquies). Asked for by the engineer, who wanted to be able to say «les moments en Nmm». The window is what made that impossible rather than merely unset: 20 kNm is 2·10⁷ Nmm, outside 0.1–1000, so a chosen unit would have been ignored exactly where it was asked for — and a report whose units depend on how big a number came out is one whose units cannot be chosen at all. Two alternatives were weighed and refused: keeping the window and letting `#unit` merely lead the candidate list (which does not do what the command says), and a hybrid where a dimension `#unit` has touched is forced while the others keep the window (more code, and a rule a reader cannot predict). ⚠️ Accepted price, and it is the merit of the old mechanism given up whole: **N/m² is one dimension**, a stress and a surface load share it, and the default serves the stress. A computed surface load therefore comes out in N/mm² unless the line says `unit: "kN/m²"` or the section says `#unit("kN/m²")` — the choice is the engineer's now rather than the value's size, which is the trade he chose knowing both halves. A test holds it, so if it ever changes it changes there first.
- *[in force]* **A command declares its named arguments one by one, settled (v0.63.1).** No public command is a bare `..args` sink (§10bis). The engineer found it by hovering `#eq` and seeing `eq(args: arguments) -> any` with no mention of `unit:` — an argument documented in the header, catalogued in §10bis's table, and invisible in the one place he was actually writing the call. Chosen over the two alternatives: leaving it and teaching the editor nothing (the header is *already* right, so the documentation was never the problem), and giving the positional tail explicit parameters too, which Typst cannot express — `#var("R_k", 7, "kN")` and `#var("q_k11", "q_(k,11)", 1.729, "kN/m²")` are the same command, and there are no optional positionals. So the split is exact: **named arguments declared, positional tail variadic**, and a test that refuses any signature which is nothing but a sink. 👉 Two things fell out of doing it. The unknown-argument check had to become a `panic` inside an `if`: Typst builds an `assert`'s message *before* testing its condition, so `repr(unknown.first())` on an empty array failed every correct call. And `->` lines were added throughout, which is what turns `-> any` into `-> content` — the fact that matters for a command that prints, since its result can go in a `grid` cell, as `Sections/005` does with `var-domain`.
- *[in force]* **A unit table lives inside the registry's dictionary, settled (v0.63).** `d.units`, beside `d.domains` and `d.stack`, rather than a `state` of its own. Not tidiness: `#eq` computes and stores its answer inside `var-registry.update(...)`, and **the function of a state's update cannot read another state** — the very constraint that put `var-domain`'s stack there in v0.61. Reading the table out of the `d` it is handed is the only way `#eq` can know what `#unit` asked for. Two consequences worth the note: the shape of a stored variable did not change, so `show-polar-moment(N: "N_Ed")`, `#val` and every existing test went on working untouched; and `lib/calc-engine.typ` needed **no edit at all**, the default table living in `formula.typ` behind `d.at("units", default: ...)` rather than in the state's initial value, which would have made calc-engine.typ know about units.
- *[in force]* **A declared unit the formula cannot reach prints in red, settled (v0.63), reversing v0.60.** It stopped the compile; it now prints the answer in the unit the operands were written in, with one red sentence naming both (§10quinquies). Asked for by the engineer, and chosen over the two alternatives with him: printing no number at all (the line then says nothing a reader can use), and converting by the raw scale factor anyway (which prints a moment labelled N — the class of error v0.60 exists to prevent). Three reasons decided it: the number printed is right either way; a report that will not build teaches nobody which of the two units is wrong; and the value stored stays physically correct, so the lines below go on computing — which a test asserts. 👉 This is a **narrow exception** to §3.6's rule that a defect blocks rather than warns, and it is the same shape as the others (§13's connection linked to no connector, a plan's unknown label): a disagreement between two units is a thing to read on the page, while a unit that cannot be *read* is a typo and still stops the compile. ⚠️ It also needed the natural unit to exist — the units the operands were written in, tracked beside the dimensions through every operation — which is new in v0.63 and composed literally (`kNm/mm` where they are mixed), the engineer's own choice over normalising them.
- *[in force]* **`var-unit`, not `change-unit`, settled (v0.63).** The engineer named it: a command whose display behaves like `#var` should say so in its name, and its line *is* `#var`'s line. It takes a **string key** like every other command of the registry, for the reason §10quinquies gives once and for all — Typst gives a function no way to bind a name in its caller's scope. ⚠️ Its refusals are raised in its `context` and not in its `state.update`, and that is a real property of this design rather than a detail of this command: the function of an update runs only where something reads that state, so a `panic` written there is **silent** in a document that never looks the registry up again — which is exactly the document a mistyped name produces. Found by a test that expected a refusal and got a clean compile.
- *[in force]* **A unit given to `#var` converts rather than relabels, settled (v0.63).** `#var("F", var-of(...), "N")` on a value the export holds in kN prints 7000 N; until v0.63 it replaced the label and left the number alone. Chosen by the engineer over refusing the call and sending him to `#var-unit`: the conversion is the obvious reading of what he wrote, and now that the engine can convert there is no reason for the other. It was a latent defect of v0.61 that nobody had hit — silently wrong, on a value read from the workbook, which is the one class of error this file exists to prevent.
- *[in force]* **Big numbers are printed as a power of ten, and only where a hand writes them, settled (v0.63).** Past 99 999, `1.235 · 10⁶` (§10quinquies). The engineer set the threshold. The scope was the question: `fmt-decimals` is the primitive **every** display path goes through, so putting the rule there would have rewritten numbers on pages nobody asked about — a connection's I_p = 157000 mm² among them — and the choice was made to confine it to `lib/formula.typ`, which reaches `#var`, `#eq`, `#verify`, `#val` and `#var-unit` and nothing else. Accepted price: two small formatting helpers that shadow `fmt-number` and `quantity-in-formula` for this file only. A test asserts `fmt-decimals(157000, 3)` is still `"157000"`, which is what keeps the confinement a fact rather than an intention. ⚠️ The exponent is written with superscript **digits**, never `10^6`: these strings are printed inside quoted words in math, where a caret is a caret — the same rule that the N² of the display bug broke.
- *[in force]* **Levels follow the report, settled (v0.61).** `level: auto` by default on `var`, `eq` and `verify`, resolved by `resolve-level` like every other command. The engineer had written "par défaut: level 3"; that would have printed every variable and every substitution in a client report compiled at level 1, and §3.7's three reports would no longer apply to these lines. What he asked for is kept exactly where it belongs — a `level:` on a call forces that one line, which is how `#var("k_dens", 0.806, level: 1)` stores a coefficient without printing it.
- *[in force]* **A second library file, settled (v0.60).** `lib/formula.typ` holds the unit algebra and the expression parser — four hundred lines `calc-engine.typ` has no other use for. It imports the three display helpers it prints through from `calc-engine.typ`, which knows nothing of it, and `commands.typ` imports both with a wildcard: the arrangement §10bis reserved for exactly this. Chosen over appending it to `calc-engine.typ`, which was already 1 838 lines, and over a shared third file, which would have moved working code to no purpose.
- *[in force]* **A check lives in the repository, settled (v0.59).** Every verification is written into `tests/`, run with `python tests/run_tests.py`, and kept (§11ter). Until v0.59 they were written and thrown away, which made every claim in `CHANGELOG.md` unrepeatable — and for a project whose argument is traceability at ten years (§1bis), the proof that the engine still reads the norm correctly was the one thing not versioned. Chosen with no framework: the runner is standard library, and `pytest` finds the same files unchanged where it is installed. Accepted price: a runner to maintain, about a hundred lines of it, against a fourth dependency to install before a report can be checked.
- *[in force]* **A rule refuses rather than warns, even when it stops today's build, settled (v0.59).** The two permanent-load rules (§3.3ter) block the export, and the first one to be written found a real defect in the engineer's own workbook and stopped the report until Excel is fixed. That is the treatment §3.6 already prescribes — "a blocking error, not a silent warning" — and the alternative was considered and refused: a warning about a load that is 21% out is a warning nobody reads twice. The exceptions stay what they were, and they are all the same shape: a stop that would also stop the export Woodcore needs to repair the thing (a connection linked to no connector, a plan's unknown label, a static model's unresolved variable). Nothing like that applies here — the fix is in Excel and needs no export to find it.
- *[in force]* **The versions of the tools are pinned, and Woodcore watches them, settled (v0.59).** `requirements.txt` states the three packages exactly (§11ter). Chosen over a floor (`>=`): §1bis's argument about norm editions is the same argument, and a report rebuilt in 2031 with a different openpyxl is not the report that was verified. Woodcore checks the machine against the file on every start — offline, instant — and asks PyPI for newer versions in a thread that may fail silently. Accepted price: a pinned version goes stale, which is exactly what the banner is for; and an update is two actions in one, installing *and* rewriting the pin, since doing only the first recreates the mismatch from the other side.
- *[in force]* **A module with no caller is deleted, not kept warm, settled (v0.59), reversing v0.11.** `domains/bending.py` and `domains/combinations.py` had stood uncalled since v0.11, for 47 versions, on the argument that they were "unchanged and still correct". They were not: both carried norm values hardcoded (`f_m_k = 24`, `gamma_M = 1.25`, `k_mod = 0.9`, `gamma_G = 1.35`, `gamma_Q = 1.5`) where §11 says a norm value lives in `norms/` and nowhere else, and both had their labels in English where the glossary is keyed by German (§4bis) — so the day they were woken they would have been woken wrong, silently. The engineer chose to delete them and write them again when the calculation is actually needed. Accepted price: the ELU combination has to be written from the clause rather than edited from a sketch, which is the point.
- *[in force]* **Load combinations centralized, settled.** ULS/SLS combinations are calculated exclusively in `calc/domains/combinations.py` — Typst only displays the already-traced result, never combination logic in the report itself.
- *[in force]* **No shared section catalog, settled (v0.10).** `data/sections.json`, `schemas/sections.schema.json`, and `calc/sections/` are gone. Every position carries its own `type`/`material`/`dimension` directly (§7); a geometry-derived value like the elastic section modulus is computed as a traced step inside the domain that needs it, not looked up from a catalog.
- *[in force]* **`dimension` is descriptive text, settled (v0.14).** The `Positions` sheet's cross-section column is copied into the position record verbatim and never parsed — no `{b, h, unit}` object, no unit field, no `DIMENSION_COLUMN` constant.
